Perturbations of a Vector Bundle whose Curvature Form Solves a Polynomial Equation
Rémi Delloque

Abstract
We investigate the behaviour of local perturbations of a wide class of geometric PDEs on holomorphic Hermitian vector bundles over a compact complex manifold. Our main goal is to study the existence of solutions near an initial solution under small deformations of both the holomorphic structure of the bundle and the parameters of the equation. Inspired by techniques from geometric invariant theory and the moment map framework, under suitable assumptions on the initial solution, we establish a local Kobayashi–Hitchin correspondence. A perturbed bundle admits a solution to the equation if and only if it satisfies a local polystability condition. We also show additional results, such as continuity and uniqueness of solutions when they exist, and a local version of the Kempf–Ness theorem. We also provide a local version of the Jordan–Hölder and Harder–Narasimhan filtrations.



1
Introduction

Motivations.

Let
X
be a connected compact complex manifold of dimension
n
. Several important complex geometric partial differential equations (PDEs) can be written as a polynomial in its unknown (with respect to the wedge product), a closed
(1,1)
-form. It is the case, for example, of the complex Monge–Ampère equation,

\chi^{n}=\eta,\qquad\chi>0,\qquad\chi\in[\chi_{0}],

the
J
-equation,

c\chi^{n}-\omega\wedge\chi^{n-1}=0,\qquad\chi>0,\qquad\chi\in[\chi_{0}],

or the deformed Hermitian Yang–Mills (dHYM) equation,

\Im\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\theta}(\omega+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\chi)^{n}\right)=0,\qquad\chi\in[\chi_{0}].

Moreover, if there is a complex line bundle
L\rightarrow X
such that
c_{1}(L)=[\chi_{0}]
, by Chern–Weil theory, the condition
\chi\in[\chi_{0}]
is equivalent to saying that
\chi
is the reduced curvature form of an integrable Chern connection
\nabla
on
L

i.e.

\chi=-\frac{1}{2\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\pi}\nabla\circ\nabla\in\Omega^{1,1}(X,\mathbb{R}).

Similarly, the Hermitian Yang–Mills (HYM) equation is

\omega^{n-1}\wedge\hat{F}=c\omega^{n},

where
\hat{F}
is the reduced curvature form of a given holomorphic Hermitian vector bundle
\mathcal{E}=(E,h,\overline{\partial})\rightarrow X
. With that in mind, a higher rank version of the three equations above has been introduced recently : by Collins and Yau for the dHYM equation
[
12
, Paragraph 8.1]
, by Pingali for the Monge–Ampère equation
[
40
]
and by Takahashi for the
J
-equation
[
46
]
. In general, following
[
41
]
, when we say that
\mathcal{E}=(E,h,\overline{\partial})
admits a solution to a polynomial equation in its curvature, we mean that there is a Dolbeault operator
\overline{\partial}^{\prime}
on the gauge orbit of
\overline{\partial}
such that the curvature of the Chern connection of
(E,h,\overline{\partial}^{\prime})
solves the equation. Here, we consider the gauge group

\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}(E)=\{f\in\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))|\forall x\in X,f_{x}\in\mathrm{GL}(E_{x})\},

acting on Dolbeault operators as follows,

f\cdot\overline{\partial}=f\circ\overline{\partial}\circ f^{-1}.

The ultimate goal with these equations is to be able to give a necessary and sufficient condition for the existence of solutions in terms of numerical invariants. On line bundles, such a condition was found for the Monge–Ampère equation by Yau
[
48
]
and for the
J
-equation by G. Chen
[
6
, Theorem 1.1]
before being improved by Song
[
43
]
. On general vector bundles, the only known numerical condition of this kind concerns the HYM equation and is imposed by geometric invariant theory (GIT). This condition is the slope stability, a result known as the Kobayashi–Hitchin correspondence, proven by Donaldson on surfaces
[
19
]
and by Uhlenbeck and Yau in higher dimensions
[
47
]
. See
[
34
]
for a survey.

For the dHYM equation (on line bundles), the expected numerical condition is the Collins–Yau–Jacob conjecture
[
10
]
. It was solved by G. Chen in the super-critical regime
[
6
, Theorem 1.7]
(after a slight reformulation of the conjecture) but it does not hold outside of this regime
[
6
, Remark 1.10]
. G. Chen’s correspondence was later improved by Chu, Lee and Takahashi
[
8
, Theorem 1.2]
and by Ballal in the projective case
[
3
, Corollary 1]
.

For a general polynomial equation on the curvature, especially in higher rank, it seems too ambitious to expect such a numerical condition to hold. The idea of this paper is to start from a Hermitian holomorphic vector bundle
\mathcal{E}_{0}\rightarrow X
whose curvature satisfies a polynomial equation in which the coefficients are closed forms and to study local perturbations of this bundle and of the coefficients of the equation. We study the impact of these deformations on the existence of solution and their behaviour when they exist. We call such equations the
P
-critical equations
.

This idea is mostly inspired from the work of Dervan, McCarthy and Sektnan
[
18
]
, and Buchdahl and Schumacher
[
5
]
. In
[
5
]
, the equation solved by
\mathcal{E}_{0}
is the HYM equation and only the complex structure of the bundle is perturbed. Buchdahl and Schumacher associate to each small perturbation of
\mathcal{E}_{0}
a point in a finite dimensional vector space on which the automorphisms group of
\mathcal{E}_{0}
acts linearly. The origin represents
\mathcal{E}_{0}
. It is shown that any small enough perturbation of
\mathcal{E}_{0}
admits a solution to the HYM equation if and only if the associated orbit is closed, providing a link between a geometric PDE and a GIT condition. This technique is inspired from Székelyhidi on cscK equation
[
45
]
.

In
[
18
]
, Dervan, McCarthy and Sektnan consider a slope semi-stable bundle
\mathcal{E}
. Thus, the sheaf
\mathcal{E}_{0}=\mathrm{Gr}(\mathcal{E})
(which is assumed to be locally free) solves the HYM equation with the right choice of complex structure. This means that
\mathcal{E}
is an infinitesimal perturbation of
\mathcal{E}_{0}
. In other words, the orbit built by Buchdahl and Schumacher contains the origin in its closure. But the coefficients of the equation vary, and the HYM equation is seen as a ”large volume limit” of a wide class of equations : the
Z
-critical equations. Dervan, McCarthy and Sektnan give a necessary and sufficient numerical condition for
\mathcal{E}
to admit solutions to these equations when the volume is large enough : the asymptotic
Z
-stability. This is an asymptotic stability condition for
\mathcal{E}
with respect to its sub-bundles which destabilise
\mathcal{E}_{0}
. Dervan, McCarthy and Sektnan generalise a previous work by Leung
[
32
]
where the asymptotic
Z
-stability corresponds to the Gieseker stability
[
28
, Sub-section 4.2]
,
[
15
, Example 3.13]
.

Outside of the large volume limit, there are a few known results on general polynomial equations. In
[
28
]
, Keller and Scarpa find necessary conditions for the existence of sub-solutions and of solutions on vector bundles of rank
2
on surfaces ; namely
P
-positivity and
P
-stability
[
28
, Theorem 1.1]
. They conjecture that the converse should hold, at least under suitable additional assumptions on the bundle
[
28
, Conjecture 1.4]
. In
[
41
]
, Napame, Scarpa, Tipler and I study
P
-positivity and
P
-stability of equivariant sheaves on
G
-varieties. We show that in some cases,
P
-positivity and
P
-stability are equivalent to their equivariant analogue
[
41
, Theorem 1.9, Theorem 1.11]
. We also study how
P
-positivity of bundles is affected by blow-ups of points
[
41
, Theorem 1.13]
.

Method.

Atiyah and Bott noticed that the HYM equation has an infinite dimensional moment map interpretation
[
2
]
. It is not the only geometric PDE which admits such an interpretation. For example, Fujiki shows a moment map interpretation for the constant scalar curvature (cscK) equation
[
23
]
, see also
[
20
]
. X. Chen and Sun use this infinite dimensional moment map interpretation in this context
[
7
]
to study small deformations of cscK manifolds. Later, Georgoulas, Robbin and Salamon wrote an excellent survey
[
24
]
on the properties of moment maps and their flows in a general compact finite dimensional setting. Most of the GIT results of this paper either come from this book, or are proved by adapting a proof from this book.

Dervan and Hallam gave a large class of geometric PDEs that admit a moment map interpretation
[
17
]
. However, in this class, the closed
(1,1)
-form associated to the moment map may be degenerate. In the case of the
P
-critical equation, the same moment map interpretation exists with the same degeneration issue. In
[
18
]
, the points (here, the Dolbeault operators) where this
(1,1)
-form is non-degenerate are called sub-solutions. We use the same terminology here. This sub-solution condition is open (with the right topology) so in our local perturbation problem, the
(1,1)
-form will be a Kähler form, yielding a usual moment map interpretation. This sub-solution condition is crucial so we require it in our definition of
P
-critical bundles.

The HYM equations have this particularity that all Dolbeault operators
\overline{\partial}
are sub-solutions. It is probably the reason why they are the most understood among
P
-critical equations. Actually, the Kobayashi–Hitchin correspondence can be seen as an infinite dimensional version of the Hilbert–Mumford criterion in the polystable case
[
24
, Theorem 12.5]
.

Moreover, as in
[
5
]
, we use the Kuranishi slice to see the set of small perturbations of
\mathcal{E}_{0}
as a germ of analytic subset of a finite dimensional vector space. This enables us to avoid the complications caused by infinite dimensional constructions. Moreover, we deform the Kuranishi slice as in
[
22
,
45
,
42
,
39
,
38
]
in the context of the cscK equation or
[
9
,
14
]
in the context of the HYM equation, to make sure that the zeroes of the moment map we build yield solutions to the
P
-critical equation.

Main results.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{0}=(E,h,\overline{\partial}_{0})\rightarrow X
be a holomorphic Hermitian vector bundle,
\nabla
its Chern connection and

\hat{F}=-\frac{1}{2\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\pi}\nabla\circ\nabla

its reduced curvature form. Assume that it satisfies a polynomial equation of the form

\sum_{k=0}^{n}\zeta_{0,k}\wedge\hat{F}^{k}=0,

where for all
k
,
\zeta_{0,k}
is a closed real
(n-k,n-k)
-form. Assume moreover that the sub-solution condition is verified (see Definition
2.1
). We call this equation the
P_{\zeta_{0}}
-critical equation
to emphasize the dependency in
\zeta
. We also say that
\mathcal{E}_{0}
is
P_{\zeta_{0}}
-critical. The formalism of
P
-critical equations was first introduced by Scarpa
[
41
, Sub-section 1.1]
.

We first show a result which is well-known in the case of the HYM equation.

Proposition 1

(Proposition
2.10
)
.

We have an orthogonal and holomorphic decomposition

\mathcal{E}_{0}=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{l}\mathcal{G}_{0,k}^{m_{k}},

where the
m_{k}\geqslant 1
are integers and the
\mathcal{G}_{0,k}
are simple
P_{\zeta_{0}}
-critical Hermitian holomorphic vector bundles. Moreover, for all
i\neq j
,
\mathrm{Hom}(\mathcal{G}_{0,i},\mathcal{G}_{0,j})=0
.

We show that the existence of a sub-solution on a bundle implies the existence of a positive
(n-1,n-1)
class in
X
, see Proposition
2.4
. In particular, there is a metric on
X
such that the associated
(1,1)
-form
\omega
is balanced
i.e.

d\omega^{n-1}=0
. Let
V=H^{0,1}(X,\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{E}_{0}))
be the complex finite dimensional space of harmonic
(0,1)
-forms with respect to
\omega
. Then, the automorphisms group of
\mathcal{E}_{0}
acts linearly on
V
and any small deformation of
\mathcal{E}_{0}
has a corresponding orbit in
V
. Let
\mathcal{E}_{b}=(E,\overline{\partial}_{b})
be the bundle represented by
b\in V
. Unless it is mentioned, the Dolbeault operator is
a priori
not integrable, so
\mathcal{E}_{b}
may not be a holomorphic bundle, but the results of this paper still apply. Let for all
\zeta=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\zeta_{k}
and all smooth complex vector bundle
F
,

P_{\zeta}(F)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}k![\zeta_{k}]\cup\mathrm{ch}_{k}(F).

Chern–Weil theory implies that
P_{\zeta}(E)=0
(see Lemma
2.3
). We then define a notion analogous to the slope (semi-)(poly)stability : the
local
P_{\zeta}
-(semi-)(poly)stability
.

Definition

(Definition
4.11
)
.

\mathcal{E}_{b}
is locally
P_{\zeta}
-semi-stable if for all smooth bundles
0\subsetneq F\subsetneq E
such that
(F,\overline{\partial}_{b})\subset\mathcal{E}_{b}
,
(F,\overline{\partial}_{0})\subset\mathcal{E}_{0}
and
(F^{\bot},\overline{\partial}_{0})\subset\mathcal{E}_{0}
are holomorphic embeddings, we have

P_{\zeta}(F)\leqslant 0.

(1)

If is said to be locally
P_{\zeta}
-stable if (
1
) is always strict. It is said to be locally
P_{\zeta}
-polystable if (
1
) is strict or
(F^{\bot},\overline{\partial}_{b})\subset\mathcal{E}_{b}
is holomorphic.

The next theorem can be seen as a local Kobayashi–Hitchin like correspondence. It confirms a local version of a conjecture of Keller and Scarpa
[
28
, Conjecture 1.4]
. Their conjecture asserts that the existence of a sub-solution is related to a kind of positivity with respect to the sub-varieties of
X
, called
Z
-positivity
[
28
, Definition 1.2]
. Under this assumption, the existence of a solution is conjectured to be related to a kind of stability with respect to the sub-sheaves of the bundle, called
Z
-stability
[
28
, Definition 1.2]
. They use the formalism of
Z
-critical equations but their results apply to any
P
-critical equation.

Theorem 2

(Theorem
4.12
)
.

If
\zeta
is close enough to
\zeta_{0}
and
b
is close enough to
0
,
\mathcal{E}_{b}
admits a solution to the
P_{\zeta}
-critical equation close to
\overline{\partial}_{0}
if and only if it is locally
P_{\zeta}
-polystable.

We also give a characterisation involving a moment map defined on a neighbourhood of
0
in
V
which depends continuously on
\zeta
. We don’t go into the details of what we mean by ”close” here, but it implies in particular that all the solutions we consider are sub-solutions. See Theorem
4.12
for more details. The topological considerations in Banach spaces are mainly discussed in Section
2
.

Moreover, the solutions of this equation, when they exist, are locally unique modulo a unitary gauge transform where the unitary gauge group is

\mathcal{G}(E,h)=\{u\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}(E)|uu^{\dagger}=\mathrm{Id}_{E}\}\subset\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}(E).

And the
\mathcal{G}(E,h)
-orbit of the solution, when it exists, varies continuously with
\zeta
and
b
.

Theorem 3

(Theorem
6.2
)
.

If
\zeta
is close enough to
\zeta_{0}
and
\overline{\partial}
and
\overline{\partial}^{\prime}
are two solutions to the
P_{\zeta}
-critical equation close enough to
\overline{\partial}_{0}
and in the same
\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}(E)
-orbit, then, they are in the same
\mathcal{G}(E,h)
-orbit.

Theorem 4

(Theorem
7.2
)
.

When it exists, the
\mathcal{G}(E,h)
-orbit of any
P_{\zeta}
-critical operator
\overline{\partial}
in the gauge orbit of
\overline{\partial}_{b}
varies continuously with respect to
\zeta
and
b
.

Once more, the topologies we use to define the continuity are given precisely in Theorem
7.2
. Theorems
2
and
4
are powerful tools to build canonical metrics as deformations of an initial special metric. They generalise
[
46
, Theorem 1.4]
and
[
46
, Theorem 1.6]
on the
J
-equation,
[
40
, Theorem 4.1]
on the Monge–Ampère equation,
[
18
, Theorem 1.1]
on the asymptotic
Z
-critical equations (which are deformations of the HYM equation),
[
9
, Theorem 1.2]
and
[
14
, Theorem 3]
on the HYM equation and
[
28
, Proposition 3.16]
on the
Z
-critical equation.

Then, in the case where
\zeta=\zeta_{0}
, we obtain a generalisation of the results of Buchdahl and Schumacher
[
5
]
. We still assume that
b
is close to
0
.

Theorem 5

(Theorem
4.13
)
.

If
\zeta=\zeta_{0}
,
\mathcal{E}_{b}
is locally
P_{\zeta_{0}}
-semi-stable. It is locally
P_{\zeta_{0}}
-polystable if and only if the orbit of
b
in
V
with respect to the action of the automorphisms group of
\mathcal{E}_{0}
is closed.

When they exist, the solutions to the
P_{\zeta}
-critical equation are built as limits of the negative gradient flow of the moment maps on
V
. We call these flows the
moment map flows
. Still under the assumptions of
\zeta
close to
\zeta_{0}
and
b
close to
0
, if
\mathcal{E}_{b}
is only locally
P_{\zeta}
-semi-stable, it can be seen as a small deformation of a locally
P_{\zeta}
-polystable bundle arising from a Jordan–Hölder filtration.

Theorem 6

(Theorem
6.3
)
.

If
\mathcal{E}_{b}
is locally
P_{\zeta}
-semi-stable, there is a
b_{\infty}
close to
0
, which is unique modulo the action of the group of unitary automorphisms of
\mathcal{E}_{0}
such that
\mathcal{E}_{b}
is an infinitesimal deformation of
\mathcal{E}_{b_{\infty}}
and
\mathcal{E}_{b_{\infty}}
is locally
P_{\zeta}
-polystable.

Moreover,
b_{\infty}
can be reached as the limit of the moment map flows and
\mathcal{E}_{b_{\infty}}
can be constructed from a Jordan–Hölder filtration of
\mathcal{E}_{b}
. We call
\mathrm{Gr}(\mathcal{E}_{b})=\mathcal{E}_{b_{\infty}}
the
graded object of
\mathcal{E}_{b}
, which is unique up to an isomorphism.

In the general case, we can also build a unique Harder–Narasimhan filtration. See Theorem
7.1
for a precise definition.

Theorem 7

(Theorem
7.1
)
.

\mathcal{E}_{b}
admits a unique Harder–Narasimhan filtration. Moreover, the limit point
b_{\infty}
of the moment map flows represents a bundle
\mathcal{E}_{b_{\infty}}
which is the direct sum of the graded objects of the locally
P_{\zeta}
-semi-stable quotients of the Harder–Narasimhan filtration of
\mathcal{E}_{b}
.

Finally, we show a local version of the Kempf–Ness theorem, which is usually a homeomorphism between a GIT quotient and a symplectic quotient. Let
L_{\mathrm{ps}}^{\mathrm{int}}
be the set of all
(\zeta,b)
close to
(0,0)
such that
\mathcal{E}_{b}
is locally
P_{\zeta}
-polystable and
\overline{\partial}_{b}
is integrable. Recall that
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{E}_{0})
acts on
b\in V
.

Theorem 8

(Theorem
7.4
)
.

For each
\zeta
close to
\zeta_{0}
, there is a germ of homeomorphism at the origin,

\{b|(\zeta,b)\in L_{\mathrm{ps}}^{\mathrm{int}}\}/\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{E}_{0})\longrightarrow\{\overline{\partial}|\overline{\partial}\textrm{ is integrable and }(E,h,\overline{\partial})\textrm{ is $P_{\zeta}$-critical}\}/\mathcal{G}(E,h).

Moreover, they vary continuously with
\zeta
.

A more complete statement can be found later (see Theorem
7.4
) but it requires to introduce many definitions and notations to be stated properly. We restrict ourselves to this partial presentation to avoid having a too cumbersome introduction.

Organisation of the paper.

In Section
2
, we define the notions of sub-solutions and solutions to the
P
-critical equation. We study the properties of a
P
-critical bundle. In particular, we show Proposition
1
. We also study the properties of the linearisation of the
P
-critical equation. We show that under the sub-solution condition, it is a non-negative real symmetric (for a suitable Hermitian product) elliptic operator of order
2
and its kernel is exactly the space of global holomorphic sections of
\mathcal{E}
.

In Section
3
, we give more detailed examples of polynomial equations in the curvature and their appearance in the literature.

In Section
4
, we give the properties of the Kuranishi slice and we construct the deformed Kuranishi slices. We also define the associated moment maps, including the infinite dimensional moment map. We end this section with the precise statements of Theorems
2
and
5
. We also give the topological structure of the locally
P_{\zeta}
-(semi-)(poly)stable loci with Proposition
4.14
but we prove it in Section
6
.

In Section
5
, we prove all the intermediate results and technical tools we need for the next section. They are mostly GIT results as the ones we can find in
[
24
]
but with the constraint that we work on an open ball of
V
, which is non-compact.

In Section
6
, we complete the proofs of Theorems
2
and
5
. We also prove Theorems
3
,
4
and
6
.

In Section
7
, we prove Theorems
7
and
8
.

Finally, in Section
8
, we apply Theorem
2
on a concrete case. We build a bundle
\mathcal{E}_{0}
of rank
2
which solves the
J
-equation, and two infinitesimal simple deformations
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}
of
\mathcal{E}_{0}
, on the blow-up of a point in
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. We find a necessary and sufficient numerical condition for them to admit solutions to the dHYM equation with respect to a varying Kähler form, in a low volume regime.

Acknowledgements.

I thank my PhD advisor Carl Tipler for suggesting this interesting problem to me, and for his guidance and support toward its resolution. I thank Carlo Scarpa for useful discussions about the
Z
-critical/
P
-critical equations. In particular, the idea of considering any polynomial equation in the curvature instead of the ones arising from central charges only, comes back to him. I thank Annamaria Ortu for useful discussions about the moment map picture for the cscK equation. I thank Ruadhaí Dervan and Lars Martin Sektnan for useful discussions about deformations of geometric PDEs which admit a moment map interpretation and their explanations of Dervan–Hallam theory
[
17
]
. I thank Ruadhaí Dervan, Julien Keller, Annamaria Ortu, Carlo Scarpa, Lars Martin Sektnan and Carl Tipler for their feedbacks on this paper and useful references. I thank Mats Andersson and Richard Lärkäng for interesting discussions in an attempt to generalise the results of this paper to non locally free sheaves.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Framework

Let
X
be a connected compact complex manifold of dimension
n
endowed with a complex Hermitian vector bundle
(E,h)\rightarrow X
.

When
\mathcal{E}=(E,\overline{\partial},h)
is a Hermitian vector bundle on
X
endowed with a (non necessarily integrable) Dolbeault operator
\overline{\partial}:\Omega^{0}(X,E)\rightarrow\Omega^{0,1}(X,E)
, we set
\hat{F}(E,\overline{\partial},h)=-\frac{1}{2\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\pi}\nabla\circ\nabla\in\Omega^{2}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))
to be the associated reduced curvature form, where
\nabla
is the Chern connection associated with
\overline{\partial}
and
h
. We set

\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(E,\overline{\partial},h)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\zeta_{k}\wedge\hat{F}(E,\overline{\partial},h)^{k}\in\Omega^{n,n}(X,\mathrm{End}(E)),

where each
\zeta_{k}
is a closed
(n-k,n-k)
-form with values in
\mathbb{R}
. This operator was first introduced by Scarpa
[
41
, Sub-section 1.1]
. When there is no ambiguity on
E
and
h
, we may drop them from the notation. We then define

P_{\zeta}(E)=[\mathrm{tr}(\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(E,\overline{\partial},h))]=\sum_{k=0}^{n}k![\zeta_{k}]\cup\mathrm{ch}_{k}(E)\in\mathbb{R},

which only depends on the topology of
E
and is additive on short exact sequences.

We need now to define the formal derivative of
\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(E,\overline{\partial},h)
with respect to
\hat{F}(E,\overline{\partial},h)
. For this, let us introduce the symmetric product of endomorphism valued forms. Let
d_{1},\ldots,d_{m}
be integers. When
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{m}
is a permutation, we denote by
\mathrm{grsgn}(\sigma)\in\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
its graded sign
i.e.
the integer modulo
2
such that for all forms
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{m}
with values in
\mathbb{C}
and homogenous degree
\deg(\alpha_{k})=d_{k}
,

\alpha_{\sigma(1)}\wedge\cdots\wedge\alpha_{\sigma(m)}=(-1)^{\mathrm{grsgn}(\sigma)}\alpha_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\alpha_{m}.

It is uniquely defined by the parities of the
d_{k}
. Notice that it always equals
1
if the
d_{k}
are all even and it equals the usual sign of a permutation if they are all odd. When
A_{1},\ldots,A_{m}
are forms with values in
\mathrm{End}(E)
with homogenous degree
\deg(A_{k})=d_{k}
, let

[A_{1},\ldots,A_{m}]_{\mathrm{sym}}=\frac{1}{m!}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{m}}(-1)^{\mathrm{grsgn}(\sigma)}A_{\sigma(1)}\wedge\cdots\wedge A_{\sigma(m)}.

Notice that it is the usual product of the
A_{k}
if they commute. We can now define the formal derivative of
\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(E,\overline{\partial},h)
with respect to
\hat{F}(E,\overline{\partial},h)
as a linear map
\Omega^{1,1}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))\rightarrow\Omega^{n,n}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))
by

\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(E,\overline{\partial},h):\kappa\mapsto\sum_{k=1}^{n}k\zeta_{k}\wedge[\underbrace{\hat{F}(E,\overline{\partial},h),\ldots,\hat{F}(E,\overline{\partial},h)}_{k-1\textrm{ times}},\kappa]_{\mathrm{sym}}.

We abusively write
[\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(E,\overline{\partial},h)\wedge\kappa]_{\mathrm{sym}}
for
(\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(E,\overline{\partial},h))(\kappa)
. Similarly, we define a bilinear map

\Omega^{1,0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))\times\Omega^{0,1}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))\rightarrow\Omega^{n,n}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))
by

\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(E,\overline{\partial},h):(\alpha,\beta)\mapsto\sum_{k=1}^{n}k\zeta_{k}\wedge[\underbrace{\hat{F}(E,\overline{\partial},h),\ldots,\hat{F}(E,\overline{\partial},h)}_{k-1\textrm{ times}},\alpha,\beta]_{\mathrm{sym}}.

We abusively write
[\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(E,\overline{\partial},h)\wedge\beta\wedge\gamma]_{\mathrm{sym}}
for
(\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(E,\overline{\partial},h))(\beta,\gamma)
. We hope no confusion occurs about the fact that in general,
[\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(E,\overline{\partial},h)\wedge\beta\wedge\gamma]_{\mathrm{sym}}\neq[\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(E,\overline{\partial},h)\wedge(\beta\wedge\gamma)]_{\mathrm{sym}}
. These quite convoluted expressions will appear in the linearisation of the
P
-critical equation
[
18
, Section 2.3.1]
.

Definition 2.1

(
[
18
, Definition 2.35]
,
[
28
, Definition 2.6]
,
[
41
, Definition 2.1]
)
.

We say that
\overline{\partial}
is a
sub-solution of the
P
-critical equation
if for all points
x\in X
and all non-zero tangent vectors
v\in T_{x}^{1,0}X\otimes\mathrm{End}(E_{x})\backslash\{0\}
,

\mathrm{tr}([\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(E,\overline{\partial},h)\wedge\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}v\wedge v^{\dagger}]_{\mathrm{sym}})>0,

where
v^{\dagger}\in T_{x}^{0,1}X\otimes\mathrm{End}(E_{x})
is the adjoint of the endomorphism
v
with respect to the Hermitian product
h_{x}
.

Definition 2.2

(
[
41
, Sub-section 1.1]
)
.

We say that
\overline{\partial}
is a
solution of the
P
-critical equation
if it is a sub-solution and

\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(E,\overline{\partial},h)=0.

We also say that
\mathcal{E}
is
P
-critical in this case, or
P_{\zeta}
-critical to emphasize the dependence in
\zeta
.

Remark
.

In some papers like
[
18
,
28
]
, being a sub-solution is not a requirement for being a solution. In this paper, it is a crucial assumption so we add it to the definition of
P
-critical bundles. Soon enough, we will consider a solution
\overline{\partial}_{0}
to this equation and look for other solutions in a close neighbourhood of
\overline{\partial}_{0}
. Therefore, the sub-solution condition, which is open, will always be satisfied.

Notice that this equality imposes a condition on the topology of
E
and on the de Rham classes of the
\zeta_{k}
.

Lemma 2.3
.

If
(E,h)
admits a solution to the
P
-critical equation, then
P_{\zeta}(E)=0
.

Proof.

Indeed, when we integrate the trace of the equality
\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(E,\overline{\partial},h)=0
, we obtain

0=\int_{X}\mathrm{tr}(\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(E,\overline{\partial},h))=P_{\zeta}(E).

∎

Notice also that the existence of a sub-solution on
E
imposes a condition on
X
and a topological condition on
E
.

Proposition 2.4

(
[
28
, Lemma 3.3]
)
.

If
(E,h)
admits a sub-solution
\overline{\partial}
to the
P
-critical equation, then
X
is balanced i.e. it admits a Hermitian metric
g
whose associated
(1,1)
-form
\omega
verifies
d\omega^{n-1}=0
. Moreover, we may choose it so

\omega^{n-1}\in\sum_{k=1}^{n}k![\zeta_{k}]\cup\mathrm{ch}_{k-1}(E).

In particular, this class is positive.

Proof.

Set

\Theta=\mathrm{tr}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}k\zeta_{k}\wedge\hat{F}(E,\overline{\partial},h)^{k-1}\right).

\Theta
is closed and if
\overline{\partial}
is a sub-solution, for all
x\in X
and all
v\in T_{x}^{1,0}X\backslash\{0\}
,

\displaystyle\Theta\wedge\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}v\wedge\overline{v}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{tr}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}k\zeta_{k}\wedge\hat{F}(E,\overline{\partial},h)^{k-1}\wedge\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}v\mathrm{Id}_{E}\wedge(v\mathrm{Id}_{E})^{\dagger}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{tr}([\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(E,\overline{\partial},h)\wedge\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}v\mathrm{Id}_{E}\wedge(v\mathrm{Id}_{E})^{\dagger}]_{\mathrm{sym}})>0.

We used the fact that homotheties commute with any endomorphism. We deduce that
\Theta
is a positive
(n-1,n-1)
-form. By the usual theorems on balanced manifolds
[
36
]
, there is a unique Hermitian metric
g
on
X
whose associated
(1,1)
-form
\omega
verifies
\omega^{n-1}=\Theta
, hence
g
is balanced. Clearly,
[\Theta]=\sum_{k=1}^{n}k![\zeta_{k}]\cup\mathrm{ch}_{k-1}(E)
.
∎

From now on, fix a balanced metric
g
on
X
and call
\omega
the associated
(1,1)
-form and
\mathrm{Vol}=\frac{\omega^{n}}{n!}
the associated volume form.

In general, when given a Hermitian vector bundle equipped with a (not necessarily integrable) Dolbeault operator
(E,\overline{\partial},h)
, we are interested by Dolbeault operators
\overline{\partial}^{\prime}
which are solutions to the
P
-critical equation and such that
(E,\overline{\partial}^{\prime})
is isomorphic to
(E,\overline{\partial})
. This is equivalent to saying that they lie in the same orbit under the action of the complex gauge group of
E

\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}(E)=\{f\in\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))|\forall x\in X,f_{x}\in\mathrm{GL}(E_{x})\},

given by,

f\cdot\overline{\partial}=f\circ\overline{\partial}\circ f^{-1}=\overline{\partial}+f\overline{\partial}(f^{-1}).

This action preserves integrable connections. We also introduce the unitary gauge group of
(E,h)
,

\mathcal{G}(E,h)=\{u\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}(E)|uu^{\dagger}=\mathrm{Id}_{E}\},

where
u^{\dagger}
is the adjoint of
u
with respect to the metric
h
.
\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}(E)
can be seen as a formal complexification of
\mathcal{G}(E,h)
.

2.2
L^{2}
Hermitian products and elliptic linearisation

From now on, we fix a Hermitian complex vector bundle
(E,h)
on
X
.

When
W
is a space of smooth sections, we call
L_{d}^{p}(W)
its
L_{d}^{p}
Sobolev completion,
\mathcal{C}^{d}(W)
its
\mathcal{C}^{d}
completion and
\mathcal{C}^{d,\alpha}(W)
its Hölder
\mathcal{C}^{d,\alpha}
completion. We can use any background metrics to define the associated norms as they are all equivalent. Let
\mathcal{X}_{\zeta}
be the set of all Dolbeault operators
\overline{\partial}
which are sub-solutions to the
P_{\zeta}
-critical equation. It is a subset of an affine space of direction
\Omega^{0,1}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))
. The map
\overline{\partial}\mapsto\hat{F}(\overline{\partial})
is continuous from
L_{d}^{p}
to
L_{d-1}^{p}
if
d\geqslant 1
. If moreover,
(d-1)p>2n
,
L_{d-1}^{p}\subset\mathcal{C}^{0}
continuously so
\hat{F}(\overline{\partial})
varies continuously in
\mathcal{C}^{0}
thus
\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(\overline{\partial})
too. Since the required condition to be a sub-solution is a pointwise positivity condition, it makes being a sub-solution an open condition for this topology. Similarly, for all
d\geqslant 1
and all
0<\alpha\leqslant 1
,
\mathcal{X}_{\zeta}
is open for the
\mathcal{C}^{d}
and the
\mathcal{C}^{d,\alpha}
topologies.

We also define the completions
L_{d}^{p}(\mathcal{X}_{\zeta})
,
\mathcal{C}^{d}(\mathcal{X}_{\zeta})
and
\mathcal{C}^{d+\alpha}(\mathcal{X}_{\zeta})
as the set of all
\overline{\partial}
with the right regularity such that the operator
\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(\overline{\partial})
satisfies the positivity condition pointwise. It makes sense because the induced curvature is continuous in this case. Moreover, for any Dolbeault operator
\overline{\partial}_{0}
on
E
,

L_{d}^{p}(\mathcal{X}_{\zeta})\subset\overline{\partial}_{0}+L_{d}^{p}(\Omega^{0,1}(X,\mathrm{End}(E)))\textrm{ is open if }(d-1)p>2n.

And similarly,

\mathcal{C}^{d}(\mathcal{X}_{\zeta})\subset\overline{\partial}_{0}+\mathcal{C}^{d}(\Omega^{0,1}(X,\mathrm{End}(E)))\textrm{ is open if }d\geqslant 1,

\mathcal{C}^{d,\alpha}(\mathcal{X}_{\zeta})\subset\overline{\partial}_{0}+\mathcal{C}^{d,\alpha}(\Omega^{0,1}(X,\mathrm{End}(E)))\textrm{ is open if }d\geqslant 1.

Finally, we complete the gauge groups. When
(d-1)p>n
, let

L_{d+1}^{p}(\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}(E))=\{g\in L_{d+1}^{p}(\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E)))|\forall x\in X,g_{x}\in\mathrm{GL}(E_{x})\},

L_{d+1}^{p}(\mathcal{G}(E,h))=\{u\in L_{d+1}^{p}(\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}(E))|uu^{\dagger}=\mathrm{Id}_{E}\}.

Similarly, we define their
\mathcal{C}^{d+1}
and their
\mathcal{C}^{d+1,\alpha}
completions when
d\geqslant 1
and
0<\alpha\leqslant 1
.

In the rest of this section, we endow the spaces of smooth sections/forms with values in
\mathrm{End}(E)
with
L^{2}
Hermitian products. First of all, we endow the space
\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))
of smooth sections of
\mathrm{End}(E)
with the usual
L^{2}
Hermitian product.

\left<f,g\right>_{L^{2}}=\int_{X}h_{\mathrm{End}(E)}(f,g)\,\mathrm{Vol}=\int_{X}\mathrm{tr}(fg^{\dagger})\,\mathrm{Vol}.

Recall that
g^{\dagger}
is the adjoint of
g
with respect to
h
. For the space
\Omega^{1,0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))
of smooth
(1,0)
-forms of
\mathrm{End}(E)
, sub-solutions provide natural Hermitian products. Indeed, let us define

\left<\alpha,\beta\right>_{L^{2}(\overline{\partial},\zeta)}=\int_{X}\mathrm{tr}([\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(\overline{\partial})\wedge\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\alpha\wedge\beta^{\dagger}]_{\mathrm{sym}}).

It is a sesquilinear form and when
\overline{\partial}
is a sub-solution to the
P
-critical equation (with parameter
\zeta
), this product is definite positive. Moreover, it induces a norm which is equivalent to the usual
L^{2}
norms because the positivity condition on
\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}
is pointwise. Similarly, we define the scalar product on
\Omega^{0,1}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))
as

\left<\alpha,\beta\right>_{L^{2}(\overline{\partial},\zeta)}=\overline{\left<\alpha^{\dagger},\beta^{\dagger}\right>_{L^{2}(\overline{\partial},\zeta)}}=\overline{\int_{X}\mathrm{tr}([\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(\overline{\partial})\wedge\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\alpha\wedge\beta^{\dagger}]_{\mathrm{sym}})}=\int_{X}\mathrm{tr}([\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(\overline{\partial})\wedge\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\beta^{\dagger}\wedge\alpha]_{\mathrm{sym}}).

Let
\nabla=\partial+\overline{\partial}
be the Chern connection associated with
(\overline{\partial},h)
. Let us introduce operators,

\overline{\partial}_{\zeta}^{*}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle\Omega^{0,1}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))&\longrightarrow&\displaystyle\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))\\
\displaystyle\alpha&\longmapsto&\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\mathrm{Vol}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}[\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(\overline{\partial})\wedge\partial\alpha]_{\mathrm{sym}}\end{array}\right.,

\partial_{\zeta}^{*}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle\Omega^{1,0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))&\longrightarrow&\displaystyle\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))\\
\displaystyle\alpha&\longmapsto&\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathrm{Vol}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}[\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(\overline{\partial})\wedge\overline{\partial}\alpha]_{\mathrm{sym}}\end{array}\right.,

\nabla_{\zeta}^{*}=\partial_{\zeta}^{*}+\overline{\partial}_{\zeta}^{*}:\Omega^{1}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))\rightarrow\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E)).

The following lemma justifies the notation.

Lemma 2.5
.

For all
\alpha\in\Omega^{1,0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))
(resp.
\Omega^{0,1}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))
,
\Omega^{1}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))
) and all
f\in\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))
,

\left<\partial f,\alpha\right>_{L^{2}(\overline{\partial},\zeta)}=\left<f,\partial_{\zeta}^{*}\alpha\right>_{L^{2}}\quad(\textrm{resp. }\left<\overline{\partial}f,\alpha\right>_{L^{2}(\overline{\partial},\zeta)}=\left<f,\overline{\partial}_{\zeta}^{*}\alpha\right>_{L^{2}},\quad\left<\nabla f,\alpha\right>_{L^{2}(\overline{\partial},\zeta)}=\left<f,\nabla_{\zeta}^{*}\alpha\right>_{L^{2}}).

Proof.

The proof for the second case is the same as the one for the first case, and the third case is simply the sum of the first two so we only give a proof of the first equality. Let
\alpha\in\Omega^{1,0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))
and
f\in\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))
.

\displaystyle\left<\partial f,\alpha\right>_{L^{2}(\overline{\partial},\zeta)}

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\mathrm{tr}([\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(\overline{\partial})\wedge\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\partial f\wedge\alpha^{\dagger}]_{\mathrm{sym}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{X}k\zeta_{k}\wedge\mathrm{tr}([\hat{F}(\overline{\partial}),\ldots,\hat{F}(\overline{\partial}),\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\partial f,\alpha^{\dagger}]_{\mathrm{sym}})

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{X}k\zeta_{k}\wedge\mathrm{tr}([\hat{F}(\overline{\partial}),\ldots,\hat{F}(\overline{\partial}),\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}f,\partial\alpha^{\dagger}]_{\mathrm{sym}})

by integration by parts and Bianchi’s identity,

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{X}k\zeta_{k}\wedge\mathrm{tr}(f[\hat{F}(\overline{\partial}),\ldots,\hat{F}(\overline{\partial}),\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\partial\alpha^{\dagger}]_{\mathrm{sym}})

by commutativity inside the trace,

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\mathrm{tr}(f(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}[\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(\overline{\partial})\wedge\overline{\partial}\alpha]_{\mathrm{sym}})^{\dagger})

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\mathrm{tr}(f(\partial_{\zeta}^{*}\alpha)^{\dagger})\,\mathrm{Vol}

\displaystyle=\left<f,\partial_{\zeta}^{*}\alpha\right>_{L^{2}}.

∎

Then, we define symmetric order
2
differential operators on smooth sections of endomorphisms of
E
,

\Delta_{\zeta,\partial}=\partial_{\zeta}^{*}\partial,\qquad\Delta_{\zeta,\overline{\partial}}=\overline{\partial}_{\zeta}^{*}\overline{\partial},\qquad\Delta_{\zeta,\nabla}=\nabla_{\zeta}^{*}\nabla=\Delta_{\zeta,\partial}+\Delta_{\zeta,\overline{\partial}}.

Notice that since
\partial f^{\dagger}=(\overline{\partial}f)^{\dagger}
on sections, we have
\partial_{\zeta}^{*}\alpha^{\dagger}=(\overline{\partial}_{\zeta}^{*}\alpha)^{\dagger}
on
(0,1)
-forms so
\Delta_{\zeta,\partial}f^{\dagger}=(\Delta_{\zeta,\overline{\partial}}f)^{\dagger}
on sections and similarly,
\Delta_{\zeta,\overline{\partial}}f^{\dagger}=(\Delta_{\zeta,\partial}f)^{\dagger}
thus
\Delta_{\zeta,\nabla}f^{\dagger}=(\Delta_{\zeta,\nabla}f)^{\dagger}
meaning this third Laplacian is a real operator.

Moreover,
\Delta_{\zeta,\nabla}
is the linearisation of the
P
-critical equation at
\overline{\partial}
with respect to Hermitian gauge transformations.

Proposition 2.6

(
[
18
, Lemma 2.36]
)
.

For all Dolbeault operator
\overline{\partial}
, if
(d-1)p>n
, the maps

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle L_{d+1}^{p}(\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}(E))&\longrightarrow&\displaystyle L_{d-1}^{p}(\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E)))\\
\displaystyle s&\longmapsto&\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathrm{Vol}}\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{s}\cdot\overline{\partial})\end{array}\right.,

are smooth. Similarly with the
\mathcal{C}^{d}
and the
\mathcal{C}^{d,\alpha}
topologies. For all
v\in\mathrm{Lie}(\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}(E))=\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))
,

\frac{d}{ds}_{|s=0}\frac{1}{\mathrm{Vol}}\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{s}\cdot\overline{\partial})v=\frac{1}{2\pi}\Delta_{\zeta,\nabla}\Re(v)+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\left[\Im(v),\frac{1}{\mathrm{Vol}}\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(\overline{\partial})\right].

The real and imaginary part of
v
has to be understood as the Hermitian and the skew-Hermitian part (divided by
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}
) of
v
. Moreover, if
\overline{\partial}
is a sub-solution,
\Delta_{\zeta,\nabla}
is elliptic.

Proof.

The map

(s,L_{d+1}^{p}(\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}(E)))\mapsto(\hat{F}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{s}\cdot\overline{\partial}),L_{d-1}^{p}(\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}(E))),

is smooth. Therefore,
s\mapsto\frac{1}{\mathrm{Vol}}\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{s}\cdot\overline{\partial})
is smooth too with the same topologies because
L_{d-1}^{p}
spaces are stable by products when
(d-1)p>n

[
1
, Theorem 4.12]
. With the same method as in
[
18
, Section 2.3.1]
, the formula for the derivative at
0
is true when
v
is Hermitian. When
v
is skew-Hermitian,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{v}
is unitary thus
\hat{F}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{v}\cdot\overline{\partial})=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{v}\hat{F}(\overline{\partial})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-v}
hence
\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{v}\cdot\overline{\partial})=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{v}\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(\overline{\partial})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-v}
. The formula for the derivative at
v
easily follows. The proofs for the
\mathcal{C}^{d}
and the
\mathcal{C}^{d,\alpha}
topologies are similar.

The proof of the ellipticity of
\Delta_{\zeta,\nabla}
is the same as in
[
18
, Lemma 2.36]
,
[
46
, Lemma 3.3]
.
∎

Finally, we want these operators to satisfy the same kind of regularity as Laplacians. Let

\Omega^{*,*}(X,\mathbb{R})=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{n}\Omega^{k,k}(X,\mathbb{C})\cap\Omega^{2k}(X,\mathbb{R}),

be the space of real smooth forms of degrees
(k,k)
. As before, we may complete it with
L_{d}^{p}
,
\mathcal{C}^{d}
or
\mathcal{C}^{d,\alpha}
topologies.

Proposition 2.7
.

The operators
\Delta_{\zeta,\partial}
,
\Delta_{\zeta,\overline{\partial}}
and
\Delta_{\zeta,\nabla}
extend continuously as

\Delta_{\zeta,\partial},\Delta_{\zeta,\overline{\partial}},\Delta_{\zeta,\nabla}:L_{d+1}^{p}(\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E)))\rightarrow L_{d-1}^{p}(\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))),

as long as
(d-1)p>2n
,
\zeta
is
L_{d-1}^{p}
and
\overline{\partial}
is
L_{d}^{p}
. Moreover, the maps

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle L_{d-1}^{p}(\Omega^{*,*}(X,\mathbb{R}))\times(\overline{\partial}_{0}+L_{d}^{p}(\Omega^{0,1}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))))&\longrightarrow&\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}(L_{d+1}^{p}(\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))),L_{d-1}^{p}(\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))))\\
\displaystyle(\zeta,\overline{\partial})&\longmapsto&\displaystyle\Delta_{\zeta,\partial},\Delta_{\zeta,\overline{\partial}},\Delta_{\zeta,\nabla}\end{array}\right.

(2)

are smooth. Similarly, we can extend them continuously as

\Delta_{\zeta,\partial},\Delta_{\zeta,\overline{\partial}},\Delta_{\zeta,\nabla}:\mathcal{C}^{d+1}(\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E)))\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{d-1}(\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))),

and

\Delta_{\zeta,\partial},\Delta_{\zeta,\overline{\partial}},\Delta_{\zeta,\nabla}:\mathcal{C}^{d+1,\alpha}(\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E)))\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{d-1,\alpha}(\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))).

as long as
d\geqslant 1
,
0<\alpha\leqslant 1
,
\zeta
is
\mathcal{C}^{d-1}
(resp.
\mathcal{C}^{d-1,\alpha}
) and
\overline{\partial}
is
\mathcal{C}^{d}
(resp.
\mathcal{C}^{d,\alpha}
) and we have the same smooth maps as in (
2
). Moreover, if
\zeta
is closed and
\overline{\partial}
is a sub-solution, these operators induce isomorphisms from their coimage toward their image with the same topologies as above.

Proof.

We have nine cases to verify but the proofs are exactly the same so we only prove the proposition for

\Delta_{\zeta,\partial}:L_{d+1}^{p}(\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E)))\rightarrow L_{d-1}^{p}(\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))).

First of all, let
\overline{\partial}_{0}
be a smooth Dolbeault operator on
E
and
\mathcal{D}=\overline{\partial}_{0}+\Omega^{0,1}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))
the affine space of Dolbeault operators, and let us introduce

q:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle L_{d}^{p}(\mathcal{D})&\longrightarrow&\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}(L_{d+1}^{p}(\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))),L_{d-1}^{p}(\Omega^{1,1}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))))\\
\displaystyle\overline{\partial}=\overline{\partial}_{0}+\alpha&\longmapsto&\displaystyle\overline{\partial}\partial=(\overline{\partial}_{0}+[\alpha,\cdot])(\partial_{0}-[\alpha^{\dagger},\cdot])\end{array}\right.

We must be careful that the
\overline{\partial}
on the left is a Dolbeault operator on
E
and the
\partial
and the
\overline{\partial}
on the right are operators on
\mathrm{End}(E)
hence the appearance of the Lie bracket with
\alpha
.

Recall that the condition
dp>2n
implies that the
L_{d}^{p}
spaces are stable by wedge product
[
1
, Theorem 4.12]
. For all
f\in L_{d+1}^{p}(\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E)))
and all
\overline{\partial}=\overline{\partial}_{0}+\alpha\in L_{d}^{p}(\mathcal{X})
,

q(\overline{\partial})f=\overline{\partial}_{0}\partial_{0}f+[\alpha,\partial_{0}f]-\overline{\partial}_{0}[\alpha^{\dagger},f]-[\alpha,[\alpha^{\dagger},f]].

We easily verify that this
(1,1)
-form is indeed
L_{d-1}^{p}
so
q
is well defined. Moreover,
q
is quadratic in
\alpha
so it is smooth if and only if it is continuous at
\overline{\partial}_{0}
and

\displaystyle\left\|q(\overline{\partial}_{0}+\alpha)-q(\overline{\partial}_{0})\right\|_{\mathrm{Hom}(L_{d+1}^{p},L_{d-1}^{p})}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\left\|f\right\|_{L_{d+1}^{p}}=1}\left\|q(\overline{\partial}_{0}+\alpha)f-q(\overline{\partial}_{0})f\right\|_{L_{d-1}^{p}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\left\|f\right\|_{L_{d+1}^{p}}=1}\left\|[\alpha,\partial_{0}f]-\overline{\partial}_{0}[\alpha^{\dagger},f]-[\alpha,[\alpha^{\dagger},f]]\right\|_{L_{d-1}^{p}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{O}\!\left(\sup_{\left\|f\right\|_{L_{d+1}^{p}}=1}\left\|[\alpha,\partial_{0}f]\right\|_{L_{d}^{p}}+\left\|[\alpha^{\dagger},f]\right\|_{L_{d}^{p}}+\left\|[\alpha,[\alpha^{\dagger},f]]\right\|_{L_{d}^{p}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{O}\!\left(\sup_{\left\|f\right\|_{L_{d+1}^{p}}=1}\left\|\alpha\right\|_{L_{d}^{p}}\left\|f\right\|_{L_{d+1}^{p}}+\left\|\alpha\right\|_{L_{d}^{p}}\left\|f\right\|_{L_{d}^{p}}+\left\|\alpha\right\|_{L_{d}^{p}}^{2}\left\|f\right\|_{L_{d}^{p}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{O}\!\left(\left\|\alpha\right\|_{L_{d}^{p}}+\left\|\alpha\right\|_{L_{d}^{p}}^{2}\right).

This proves continuity. Notice that we only used
dp>2n
for this part. Then,

\overline{\partial}\in L_{d}^{p}(\mathcal{D})\mapsto\hat{F}(\overline{\partial})\in L_{d-1}^{p}(\Omega^{1,1}(X,\mathrm{End}(E)))

is smooth and
(d-1)p>2n
so
L_{d-1}^{p}
spaces are stable by wedge products. Therefore,

(\zeta,\overline{\partial})\in L_{d-1}^{p}(\Omega^{*,*}(X,\mathbb{R}))\times L_{d}^{p}(\mathcal{D})\mapsto\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(\overline{\partial})\in\mathrm{Hom}(L_{d-1}^{p}(\Omega^{1,1}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))),L_{d-1}^{p}(\Omega^{n,n}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))))

is smooth too. Up to a constant smooth
(n,n)
-form, the operator
\Delta_{\zeta,\partial}
is the composition if this map with
q
thus,

(\zeta,\overline{\partial})\in L_{d-1}^{p}(\Omega^{*,*}(X,\mathbb{R}))\times L_{d}^{p}(\mathcal{D})\mapsto\Delta_{\zeta,\partial}\in\mathrm{Hom}(L_{d+1}^{p}(\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))),L_{d-1}^{p}(\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))))

is well-defined and smooth.

All we have left to prove is that for all fixed
(\zeta,\overline{\partial})
, if
\zeta
is closed and
\overline{\partial}\in L_{d}^{p}(\mathcal{X}_{\zeta})
,

\Delta_{\zeta,\partial}:L_{d+1}^{p}(\mathrm{coim}(\Delta_{\zeta,\partial}))\rightarrow L_{d-1}^{p}(\mathrm{im}(\Delta_{\zeta,\partial}))

is an isomorphism. By Lemma
2.5
,
\Delta_{\zeta,\partial}
is symmetric and elliptic (in particular, Fredholm of index
0
). By Fredholm alternative, it is bijective from
L_{d+1}^{p}(\mathrm{coim}(\Delta_{\zeta,\partial}))
to
L_{d-1}^{p}(\mathrm{im}(\Delta_{\zeta,\partial}))
, and by the open mapping theorem, it is an isomorphism.
∎

2.3
Decomposition of
P
-critical bundles

In this sub-section, we show that
P
-critical bundles are, as HYM bundles, decomposable as a direct sum of simple
P
-critical bundles which share no holomorphic morphisms.

Lemma 2.8
.

If
\mathcal{E}=(E,\overline{\partial},h)
is a
P
-critical vector bundle, all its automorphisms are parallel (equivalently, automorphisms are stable by
f\mapsto f^{\dagger}
).

Proof.

Let
f\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{E})
such that
\overline{\partial}f=0
. By Proposition
2.6
, we have

\frac{d}{dt}_{|t=0}\frac{1}{\mathrm{Vol}}\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{tf}\cdot\overline{\partial})=\frac{1}{2\pi}\Delta_{\zeta,\nabla}\Re(f)+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\left[\Im(f),\frac{1}{\mathrm{Vol}}\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(\overline{\partial})\right]=\frac{1}{2\pi}\Delta_{\zeta,\nabla}\Re(f),

where we used here the fact that
\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(\overline{\partial})=0
by hypothesis. Moreover,
f
is holomorphic thus for all
t
,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{tf}\cdot\overline{\partial}=\overline{\partial}
. We deduce that
\frac{1}{2\pi}\Delta_{\zeta,\nabla}\Re(f)=0
as it is the derivative at
0
of a constant function. Therefore, by Lemma
2.5
,

\left\|\nabla\Re(f)\right\|_{L^{2}(\overline{\partial},\zeta)}=\left<\Delta_{\zeta,\nabla}\Re(f),\Re(f)\right>_{L^{2}}=0

and
\left<\cdot,\cdot\right>_{L^{2}(\overline{\partial},\zeta)}
is definite positive because
\overline{\partial}
is a sub-solution so
\nabla\Re(f)=0
. In particular,
\Re(f)
is holomorphic. So are
\Im(f)=-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}(f-\Re(f))
and
f^{\dagger}=\Re(f)-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\Im(f)
.
∎

Lemma
2.8
can be seen as the analogue in GIT of the fact that the stabiliser of a polystable point is reductive. Indeed, it implies that the complex Lie group
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{E})
of automorphisms of
\mathcal{E}
is the complexification of the compact real Lie group
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{E},h)
of unitary automorphisms with respect to
h
. In particular,
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{E})
is reductive. It also has for consequence a partial result of uniqueness of solutions.

Proposition 2.9
.

If
\overline{\partial}_{1}
and
\overline{\partial}_{2}
are solutions to the
P
-critical equation in the same
\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}(E)
-orbit, we have equivalence between,

1.

\overline{\partial}_{1}
and
\overline{\partial}_{2}
are in the same
\mathcal{G}(E,h)
-orbit.

2.

\overline{\partial}_{1}\oplus\overline{\partial}_{2}
is a sub-solution to the
P
-critical equation on
F=(E,h)\oplus(E,h)
.

Proof.

1\Rightarrow 2
Assume
\overline{\partial}_{2}=u\cdot\overline{\partial}_{1}
for some unitary transform
u
. Set
\hat{F}_{1}=\hat{F}(E,\overline{\partial}_{1})
and
\hat{F}_{2}=\hat{F}(E,\overline{\partial}_{2})
. Then,
\hat{F}_{2}=u\hat{F}_{1}u^{\dagger}
so for all
v=\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}&v_{2}\\
v_{3}&v_{4}\end{pmatrix}
in
T_{x}^{1,0}X\otimes\mathrm{End}(F_{x})
and all
i
,
j
and
k
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}(\hat{F}(F,\overline{\partial}_{1}\oplus\overline{\partial}_{2})^{i}\wedge\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}v\wedge\hat{F}(F,\overline{\partial}_{1}\oplus\overline{\partial}_{2})^{j}\wedge v^{\dagger}\wedge\hat{F}(F,\overline{\partial}_{1}\oplus\overline{\partial}_{2})^{k})

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\left(\begin{pmatrix}\hat{F}_{1}^{i}&0\\
0&\hat{F}_{2}^{i}\end{pmatrix}\wedge\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}v_{1}&\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}v_{2}\\
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}v_{3}&\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}v_{4}\end{pmatrix}\wedge\begin{pmatrix}\hat{F}_{1}^{j}&0\\
0&\hat{F}_{2}^{j}\end{pmatrix}\wedge\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}^{\dagger}&v_{3}^{\dagger}\\
v_{2}^{\dagger}&v_{4}^{\dagger}\end{pmatrix}\wedge\begin{pmatrix}\hat{F}_{1}^{k}&0\\
0&\hat{F}_{2}^{k}\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}(\hat{F}_{1}^{i}\wedge\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}v_{1}\wedge\hat{F}_{1}^{j}\wedge v_{1}^{\dagger}\wedge\hat{F}_{1}^{k})+\mathrm{tr}(\hat{F}_{1}^{i}\wedge\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}v_{2}\wedge u\hat{F}_{1}^{j}u^{\dagger}\wedge v_{2}^{\dagger}\wedge\hat{F}_{1}^{k})

\displaystyle+\

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}(u\hat{F}_{1}^{i}u^{\dagger}\wedge\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}v_{3}\wedge\hat{F}_{1}^{j}\wedge v_{3}^{\dagger}\wedge u\hat{F}_{1}^{k}u^{\dagger})+\mathrm{tr}(u\hat{F}_{1}^{i}u^{\dagger}\wedge\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}v_{4}\wedge u\hat{F}_{1}^{j}u^{\dagger}\wedge v_{4}^{\dagger}\wedge u\hat{F}_{1}^{k}u^{\dagger})

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}(\hat{F}_{1}^{i}\wedge\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}v_{1}\wedge\hat{F}_{1}^{j}\wedge v_{1}^{\dagger}\wedge\hat{F}_{1}^{k})+\mathrm{tr}(\hat{F}_{1}^{i}\wedge(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}v_{2}u)\wedge\hat{F}_{1}^{j}\wedge(v_{2}u)^{\dagger}\wedge\hat{F}_{1}^{k})

\displaystyle+\

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}(\hat{F}_{1}^{i}\wedge(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}u^{\dagger}v_{3})\wedge\hat{F}_{1}^{j}\wedge(u^{\dagger}v_{3})^{\dagger}\wedge\hat{F}_{1}^{k})+\mathrm{tr}(\hat{F}_{1}^{i}\wedge(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}u^{\dagger}v_{4}u)\wedge\hat{F}_{1}^{j}\wedge(u^{\dagger}v_{4}u)^{\dagger}\wedge\hat{F}_{1}^{k}).

We deduce that

\displaystyle[\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(F,\overline{\partial}_{1}\oplus\overline{\partial}_{2},h\oplus h)\wedge\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}v\wedge v^{\dagger}]_{\mathrm{sym}}

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle[\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(E,\overline{\partial}_{1},h)\wedge\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}v_{1}\wedge v_{1}^{\dagger}]_{\mathrm{sym}}+[\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(E,\overline{\partial}_{1},h)\wedge\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}(v_{2}u)\wedge(v_{2}u)^{\dagger}]_{\mathrm{sym}}

\displaystyle+\

\displaystyle[\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(E,\overline{\partial}_{1},h)\wedge\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}(u^{\dagger}v_{3})\wedge(u^{\dagger}v_{3})^{\dagger}]_{\mathrm{sym}}+[\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(E,\overline{\partial}_{1},h)\wedge\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}(u^{\dagger}v_{4}u)\wedge(u^{\dagger}v_{4}u)^{\dagger}]_{\mathrm{sym}}.

The result immediately follows. Notice that we also have
\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(F,\overline{\partial}_{1}\oplus\overline{\partial}_{2},h\oplus h)=0
so
\overline{\partial}_{1}\oplus\overline{\partial}_{2}
is a solution to the
P
-critical equation.

2\Rightarrow 1
Let
\overline{\partial}_{2}=f\cdot\overline{\partial}_{1}
be both
P
-critical. Let
\mathcal{E}_{1}=(E,\overline{\partial}_{1},h)
,
\mathcal{E}_{2}=(E,\overline{\partial}_{2},h)
and
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{E}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{E}_{2}
endowed with the direct sum metric and complex structure. By assumption,
\overline{\partial}_{1}\oplus\overline{\partial}_{2}
is a sub-solutions and the equality
\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(F,\overline{\partial}_{1}\oplus\overline{\partial}_{2})=0
is trivial. It makes
\mathcal{F}
a
P
-critical bundle. Let

g=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
f&0\end{pmatrix}.

Since
f:\mathcal{E}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{2}
is holomorphic, so is
g:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
. By Lemma
2.8
,
g^{\dagger}
is holomorphic and

g^{\dagger}=\begin{pmatrix}0&f^{\dagger}\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},

because the decomposition
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{E}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{E}_{2}
is orthogonal. It implies that
f^{\dagger}:\mathcal{E}_{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{1}
is holomorphic. We deduce that
\sqrt{f^{\dagger}f}:\mathcal{E}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{1}
is holomorphic too thus
\overline{\partial}_{2}=f\cdot\overline{\partial}_{1}=f\sqrt{f^{\dagger}f}^{-1}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{1}
and
f\sqrt{f^{\dagger}f}^{-1}:E\rightarrow E
is unitary.
∎

The following proposition means that a
P
-critical bundle admits a decomposition as a direct sum of
P
-critical simple components. If two of these components are isomorphic, they are orthogonally isomorphic, else, there are no holomorphic morphisms from one to an other.

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be
P
-critical. It admits an orthogonal and holomorphic decomposition

\mathcal{E}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{l}\mathcal{G}_{i}^{m_{i}},

where the
m_{i}\geqslant 1
are integers and for all
i\neq j
,
\mathrm{Hom}(\mathcal{G}_{i},\mathcal{G}_{j})=0
.

Proof.

Step 1 :

\mathcal{E}
decomposes as an orthogonal and holomorphic sum of simple sub-bundles.

Let us prove it by induction on the rank of
E
. It is trivial if
\mathrm{rk}(E)=1
. If
E
has rank
\mathrm{rk}(E)\geqslant 2
, it is also trivial if
\mathcal{E}
is simple, else,
\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{E})
admits a holomorphic section
\xi
which is not a homothety. Using Lemma
2.8
, up to replacing
\xi
by its real part or its imaginary part, we may assume that
\xi
is Hermitian and is not a homothety.

The coefficients of the characteristic polynomial of
\xi
in function of
x\in X
are holomorphic thus constant. We deduce that the eigenvalues of
\xi
and their multiplicities are constant so each eigenspace
\ker(\lambda\mathrm{Id}_{E}-\xi)
is a holomorphic sub-bundle of
\mathcal{E}
. Let
\Pi_{\lambda}\in\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))
be the orthogonal projection onto this space. By the spectral theorem, we have
\mathrm{Sp}(\xi)\subset\mathbb{R}
and

\xi=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathrm{Sp}(\xi)}\lambda\Pi_{\lambda}.

(3)

As each
\ker(\lambda\mathrm{Id}_{E}-\xi)
is a holomorphic sub-bundle of
\mathcal{E}
,
\overline{\partial}\Pi_{\lambda}
takes values in
\ker(\lambda\mathrm{Id}_{E}-\xi)
. In other words, we have
\Pi_{\lambda}\overline{\partial}\Pi_{\lambda}=\overline{\partial}\Pi_{\lambda}
and for all
\nu\neq\lambda
,
\Pi_{\nu}\overline{\partial}\Pi_{\lambda}=0
. When we differentiate (
3
), we obtain

0=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathrm{Sp}(\xi)}\lambda\overline{\partial}\Pi_{\lambda},

so for all
\nu\in\mathrm{Sp}(\xi)
,

0=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathrm{Sp}(\xi)}\lambda\Pi_{\nu}\overline{\partial}\Pi_{\lambda}=\nu\overline{\partial}\Pi_{\nu}.

Recall that
\xi
is not a homothety so we can choose
\nu\neq 0
and
\Pi_{\nu}\neq 0,\mathrm{Id}_{E}
. In this case,
\overline{\partial}\Pi_{\nu}=0
so
\Pi_{\nu}
is holomorphic, meaning that we have an exact sequence which splits both orthogonally and holomorphically,

\hbox to316.48pt{\vbox to25.48pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 158.23692pt\lower-13.19498pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{{{}}}{{{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {

{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 153.35 -0.68 L 185.45 -0.68}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{134.22586pt}{-0.49501pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Therefore,
\mathcal{E}=\ker(\nu\mathrm{Id}_{E}-\xi)\oplus\ker(\nu\mathrm{Id}_{E}-\xi)^{\bot}
and we conclude by induction. Let us write

\mathcal{E}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{l}\mathcal{G}_{i},

where the
\mathcal{G}_{i}
are the simple components of
\mathcal{E}
.

Step 2 :
Each
\mathcal{G}_{i}
is
P
-critical.

Let for all
i
,
G_{i}
be the smooth structure of
\mathcal{G}_{i}
. In the decomposition
\mathcal{E}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{l}\mathcal{G}_{i}
, we have

\hat{F}(E,\overline{\partial})=\begin{pmatrix}\hat{F}(G_{1},\overline{\partial})&0&\ldots&0\\
0&\hat{F}(G_{2},\overline{\partial})&\ldots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\ldots&\hat{F}(G_{l},\overline{\partial})\end{pmatrix},

because the decomposition is both orthogonal and holomorphic. Therefore if
x\in X
and
v\in T_{x}^{1,0}X\otimes\mathrm{End}(G_{i,x})\backslash\{0\}
, let
\tilde{v}
be its trivial extension to the whole
\mathrm{End}(E)
. Since the decomposition of
\mathcal{E}
into the sum of the
\mathcal{G}_{i}
is orthogonal,
\widetilde{(v^{\dagger})}=(\tilde{v})^{\dagger}
and

0<\mathrm{tr}([\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(E,\overline{\partial},h)\wedge\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\tilde{v}\wedge\tilde{v}^{\dagger}]_{\mathrm{sym}})=\mathrm{tr}([\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(G_{i},\overline{\partial},h)\wedge\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}v\wedge v^{\dagger}]_{\mathrm{sym}}),

so
\overline{\partial}
(restricted to
G_{i}
) is a sub-solution of the
P
-critical equation on
(G_{i},h)
and

0=\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(E,\overline{\partial},h)=\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(G_{1},\overline{\partial},h)&0&\ldots&0\\
0&\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(G_{2},\overline{\partial},h)&\ldots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\ldots&\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(G_{l},\overline{\partial},h)\end{pmatrix}.

We deduce that for all
i
,
\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(G_{i},\overline{\partial},h)=0
thus
\mathcal{G}_{i}
is
P
-critical.

Step 3 :
If
\mathrm{Hom}(\mathcal{G}_{i},\mathcal{G}_{j})\neq 0
,
\mathcal{G}_{i}
and
\mathcal{G}_{j}
are orthogonally isomorphic.

Let
1\leqslant i,j\leqslant l
. Assume there is a non-zero holomorphic
f:\mathcal{G}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}_{j}
. Using the natural inclusion
\mathrm{Hom}(\mathcal{G}_{i},\mathcal{G}_{j})\rightarrow\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{E})
and Lemma
2.8
,
f^{\dagger}:\mathcal{G}_{j}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}_{i}
is holomorphic. We deduce that
f^{\dagger}f:\mathcal{G}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}_{i}
is holomorphic. Since
f\neq 0
,
f^{\dagger}f\neq 0
and by simplicity of
\mathcal{G}_{i}
,
f^{\dagger}f=a\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{G}_{i}}
for some
a\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
.

Moreover,
f^{\dagger}f
is Hermitian positive so
a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}
. We deduce that
\frac{f}{\sqrt{a}}:\mathcal{G}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}_{j}
is a unitary isomorphism.

Step 4 :
Conclusion.

Let
1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m
. If
\mathcal{G}_{i}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{G}_{j}
, they are orthogonally isomorphic by Step 3. Therefore,
\mathcal{G}_{i}\oplus\mathcal{G}_{j}=\mathcal{G}_{i}^{2}
orthogonally and holomorphically. Else,
\mathrm{Hom}(\mathcal{G}_{i},\mathcal{G}_{j})=\mathrm{Hom}(\mathcal{G}_{j},\mathcal{G}_{i})=0
, still by Step 3. We conclude by regrouping together the pairwise isomorphic simple components of
\mathcal{E}
.
∎

Remark
.

By Lemma
2.3
, it automatically implies that for all
i
,
P_{\zeta}(G_{i})=0
.

3
Examples

3.1
The
Z
-critical equation

Assume
X
is Kähler and let
\omega
be a Kähler form on it. Let
U\in H^{*,*}(X,\mathbb{R})
with
U_{0}=1
and
\rho=(\rho_{0},\ldots,\rho_{n})
be a vector of complex numbers. Let for all
i
,
u_{i}\in U_{i}
a smooth closed form. Then, we can introduce following operators
[
18
, Definition 2.22]
,

\mathcal{Z}(E,\overline{\partial},h)=\left(\sum_{d=0}^{n}\rho_{d}\omega^{d}\wedge u\wedge\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\hat{F}(E,\overline{\partial},h)}\right)_{(n,n)},

Z(E)=\int_{X}\mathrm{tr}(\mathcal{Z}(E,\overline{\partial},h))=\left(\sum_{d=0}^{n}\rho_{d}[\omega]^{d}\wedge U\wedge\mathrm{ch}(E)\right)_{(n,n)}.

Here,
\alpha_{(n,n)}
denotes the component of bidegree
(n,n)
of a form
\alpha
. We say that
Z
is the associated
central charge
. Assume that
E
verifies
Z(E)\neq 0
and write
Z(E)=\left|Z(E)\right|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\varphi(E)}
. The associated
P
-critical operator is

\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(E,\overline{\partial},h)=\Im(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\varphi(E)}\mathcal{Z}(E,\overline{\partial},h)).

The equation
\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(E,\overline{\partial},h)=0
is the
Z
-critical equation
. By definition of
\varphi(E)
, we have
P_{\zeta}(E)=\Im(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\varphi(E)}Z(E))=0
and we can compute that the closed real forms
\zeta_{k}
are given by

\zeta_{k}=\frac{1}{\left|Z(E)\right|k!}\sum_{i+j\leqslant n}\sum_{l=0}^{n-k}\Im(\overline{\rho_{i}}\rho_{l})\left(\int_{X}[\omega]^{i}\cup U_{j}\cup\mathrm{ch}_{n-i-j}(E)\right)\omega^{l}\wedge u_{n-k-l}.

(4)

The name ”
P
-critical equation” is inspired from ”
Z
-critical equation”. All of the other examples below can be obtained as
Z
-critical equations whose parameters
(\rho,\omega,u)
don’t depend on the particular bundle
E
we are working on. However, we see in (
4
) that the parameter
\zeta
of the associated
P
-critical equation depends on the topology of
E
, which comes from the necessary condition
P_{\zeta}(E)=0
.

The only known results about
Z
-critical equations on a bundle of rank
2
or more and outside of asymptotic regimes come from Keller and Scarpa on surfaces
[
28
, Theorem 1.1]
. We can notice that any polynomial equation in the curvature can be recovered from a central charge. In particular, Keller and Scarpa’s results apply to any
P
-critical equation. They show that the existence of a sub-solution implies a form of stability of the bundle with respect to sub-varieties of dimension
1

[
28
, Definition 1.2]
. If moreover,
E
is indecomposable of rank
2
, they show that the existence of a solution implies a form of stability with respect to sub-bundles of rank
1
. They conjecture that the converse holds
[
28
, Conjecture 1.4]
.

Notice that under the condition
\Im\left(\frac{\rho_{n-1}}{\rho_{n}}\right)>0
, if we replace
\omega
by
\epsilon^{-1}\omega
with
\epsilon>0
small, the equation we obtain converges toward the HYM equation. This specific asymptotic regime know as the large volume limit, was studied by Dervan, McCarthy and Sektnan
[
18
]
.

3.2
The Hermitian Yang–Mills equation

Let
g
be a balanced metric on
X

i.e.
the associated
(1,1)
-form
\omega
verifies
d\omega^{n-1}=0
. In this case, we can set

\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(E,\overline{\partial},h)=\omega^{n-1}\wedge\hat{F}(E,\overline{\partial},h)-\frac{[\omega^{n-1}]\cup c_{1}(E)}{\mathrm{rk}(E)[\omega^{n}]}\omega^{n}\mathrm{Id}_{E}.

We immediately see that
P_{\zeta}(E)=0
. Moreover, if
x\in X
and
v\in T_{x}^{1,0}X\otimes\mathrm{End}(E_{x})\backslash\{0\}
,

\mathrm{tr}([\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(E,\overline{\partial},h)\wedge\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}v\wedge v^{\dagger}]_{\mathrm{sym}})=\omega^{n-1}\wedge\mathrm{tr}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}v\wedge v^{\dagger})>0,

so the sub-solution condition is always verified.
\overline{\partial}
is a solution to the
P
-critical equation if and only if it is HYM.

In this case, solutions are unique modulo the unitary gauge group and exist if and only if the bundle is slope polystable, as shown by Donaldson, Uhlenbeck and Yau
[
19
,
47
,
34
]
, a famous result known as the Kobayashi–Hitchin correspondence. Theorem
2
can be seen as a local version of this correspondence.

In the HYM case, Theorem
4.13
(
i.e.
when the equation does not vary), was already shown by Buchdahl and Schumacher in
[
5
]
. Notice however that their results involve a coarser topology than here, because they don’t need to worry about working with sub-solutions since all
\overline{\partial}
are sub-solutions to the HYM equation.

If we consider variations of the balanced metric
\omega^{n-1}
, which can be translated as a variation of the associated form
\zeta
, the continuity result given by Theorem
4
generalises
[
14
]
.

3.3
The complex Monge–Ampère equation

Let
\eta\in n!\mathrm{ch}_{n}(E)
be a
(n,n)
-form on
X
and

\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(E,\overline{\partial},h)=\hat{F}(E,\overline{\partial},h)^{n}-\eta.

In this case,
P_{\zeta}(E)=0
because
[\eta]=n!\mathrm{ch}_{n}(E)
. For all
x\in X
and
v\in T_{x}^{1,0}X\otimes\mathrm{End}(E_{x})
, if we set
\hat{F}=\hat{F}(E,\overline{\partial},h)
for some
\overline{\partial}
and
h
on
E
,

\mathrm{tr}([\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(E,\overline{\partial},h))\wedge\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}v\wedge v^{\dagger}]_{\mathrm{sym}})=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathrm{tr}\left(\hat{F}(E,\overline{\partial},h)^{k-1}\wedge\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}v\wedge\hat{F}(E,\overline{\partial},h)^{n-1-k}\wedge v^{\dagger}\right).

This equation is the complex Monge–Ampère equation, originally on line bundles. On a line bundle
L
, the sub-solution condition is equivalent to
\hat{F}(L,h,\overline{\partial})
being a Kähler form (thus
L
must be ample). The condition of existence is simply the ampleness of
L
(or more generally, the ampleness of the
(1,1)
-class where we look for a solution). It is the Calabi conjecture, solved by Yau
[
48
]
. See
[
11
]
for a survey.

It was later generalised by Datar and Pingali in
[
13
]
where the sub-solution condition is called the ”cone condition”. They show an equivalence between the existence of a solution, the existence of a sub-solution and a numerical condition
[
13
, Theorem 1.1]
.

In the case of bundles of rank
2
or more, The Monge–Ampère equation was introduced by Pingali
[
40
]
. He calls sub-solutions MA-positively curved
[
40
, page 2]
. The positivity properties of vector bundles which satisfy the Monge–Ampère equation are studied by Ballal and Pingali in
[
4
]
.

3.4
The
J
-equation

Assume
X
is Kähler and set
\omega
a Kähler form on
X
. Assume also that
n\geqslant 2
and
E
is a vector bundle such that
\mathrm{ch}_{n}(E)>0
and
[\omega]\cup\mathrm{ch}_{n-1}(E)>0
and let

\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(E,\overline{\partial},h)=\frac{[\omega]\cup\mathrm{ch}_{n-1}(E)}{n\mathrm{ch}_{n}(E)}\hat{F}(E,\overline{\partial},h)^{n}-\omega\wedge\hat{F}(E,\overline{\partial},h)^{n-1}.

In this case,
P_{\zeta}(E)=0
once again and for all
x\in X
and
v\in T_{x}^{1,0}X\otimes\mathrm{End}(E_{x})
, if we set
\hat{F}=\hat{F}(E,\overline{\partial},h)
for some
\overline{\partial}
and
h
on
E
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}([\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(E,\overline{\partial},h))\wedge\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}v\wedge v^{\dagger}]_{\mathrm{sym}})

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\left(\frac{[\omega]\cup\mathrm{ch}_{n-1}(E)}{n\mathrm{ch}_{n}(E)}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\hat{F}^{k}\wedge\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}v\wedge\hat{F}^{n-1-k}\wedge v^{\dagger}-\omega\wedge\sum_{k=0}^{n-2}\hat{F}^{k}\wedge\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}v\wedge\hat{F}^{n-2-k}\wedge v^{\dagger}\right).

This equation is the
J
-equation. It was originally proposed by Donaldson
[
21
]
in the case of line bundles. G. Chen proved a Nakai–Moishezon like criterion
[
6
, Theorem 1.1]
which was later improved by Song
[
43
, Corollary 1.2]
.

The
J
-equation was extended in the case of bundles of any rank by Takahashi
[
46
]
. He calls sub-solutions
J
-positive
[
46
, Definition 3.1]
.

3.5
The deformed Hermitian Yang–Mills equation

Assume once again that
X
is Kähler and set
\omega
a Kähler form on
X
. Let

\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(E,\overline{\partial},h)=\Im(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\theta}(\omega\mathrm{Id}_{E}+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\hat{F}(E,\overline{\partial},h))^{n}).

It is the dHYM equation. The constant
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
must be the argument of

\displaystyle Z_{\mathrm{dHYM}}(E)

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\mathrm{tr}((\omega\mathrm{Id}_{E}+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\hat{F}(E,\overline{\partial},h))^{n})

\displaystyle=n!\int_{X}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}^{n}\mathrm{tr}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\omega\mathrm{Id}_{E}+\hat{F}(E,\overline{\partial},h)})

\displaystyle=n!\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}^{n}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}[\omega]}\cup\mathrm{ch}(E)\right)_{(n,n)},

at least when this quantity does not vanish, so the equality
P_{\zeta}(E)=0
is verified.

Once more, the usual equation is on line bundles. It comes from SYZ mirror symmetry as the dual of the special Lagrangian equation on a Calabi–Yau manifold
[
33
,
35
]
. It was generalised by Collins and Yau
[
12
, Paragraph 8.1]
where it is expected to be the dual of the special Lagrangian of a multi-section
[
35
,
33
,
37
,
25
]
. Notice that they also require that

\Re(\mathrm{tr}((\omega\mathrm{Id}_{E}+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\hat{F}(E,\overline{\partial},h))^{n}))>0.

Even in the line bundle case, the algebraic condition of existence of a solution is still a conjecture, known as the Collins–Yau–Jacob conjecture
[
10
, Conjecture 1.4]
, which was only verified in particular cases. For example, in the super-critical phase, it was solved by G. Chen
[
6
, Theorem 1.7]
and later improved by Chu, Lee and Takahashi
[
8
, Theorem 1.2]
and by Ballal in the projective case
[
3
, Corollary 1]
. It does not hold outside of the super-critical phase
[
6
, Remark 1.10]
where it remains an open problem. See
[
11
]
for a survey.

4
Moment maps and Kuranishi slices

We know from Lemma
2.3
that a necessary topological condition for
E
to admit a solution to the
P
-critical equation is that
P_{\zeta}(E)=0
. Let us introduce

\mathcal{Z}=\{\zeta\in\Omega^{*,*}(X,\mathbb{R})|d\zeta=0,P_{\zeta}(E)=0\}.

Notice that once we assume
\zeta
is closed, the equality
P_{\zeta}(E)=0
is linear in
[\zeta]\in H^{*,*}(X,\mathbb{R})
. In particular,
\mathcal{Z}
is a real sub-space of
\Omega^{*,*}(X,\mathbb{R})
which contains
\zeta_{0}
.

Let us fix
\zeta_{0}\in\mathcal{C}^{d-1}(\mathcal{Z})
, with
d
a positive integer. Let
\mathcal{E}_{0}=(E,h,\overline{\partial}_{0})
be a
P_{\zeta_{0}}
-critical bundle. Assume moreover that
\overline{\partial}_{0}^{2}=0
so
\mathcal{E}_{0}
is actually a holomorphic vector bundle. By Proposition
2.10
, we can decompose it both orthogonally and holomorphically as

\mathcal{E}_{0}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{l}\mathcal{G}_{0,i}^{m_{i}},

where the
\mathcal{G}_{0,i}
are simple and pairwise non-isomorphic. Moreover, for all
i\neq j
,
\mathrm{Hom}(\mathcal{G}_{0,i},\mathcal{G}_{0,j})=0
. Let
G=\mathrm{Aut}_{0}(\mathcal{E}_{0})
be the group of automorphisms of
\mathcal{E}_{0}
of determinant
1
and
K=\mathrm{Aut}_{0}(\mathcal{E}_{0},h)
the compact sub-group of unitary automorphisms of determinant
1
. Let
\mathfrak{g}=\mathrm{Lie}(G)
and
\mathfrak{k}=\mathrm{Lie}(K)
.
\mathfrak{g}
is the complex space of trace-free holomorphic endomorphisms of
\mathcal{E}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{k}\subset\mathfrak{g}
is the real space of trace-free skew-Hermitian holomorphic endomorphisms of
\mathcal{E}_{0}
. By Lemma
2.8
,
\mathfrak{g}
is the complexification of
\mathfrak{k}
, thus
G
is reductive with maximal compact sub-group
K
.

This space inherits from the
L^{2}
Hermitian product on
\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))
. We simply call
\left<\cdot,\cdot\right>
the restriction. This Hermitian product is
K
-invariant. In particular, balls in
V
are preserved by the action of
K
.

Since there is no non-zero morphisms between two distinct
\mathcal{G}_{0,i}
, we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i=1}^{l}U(m_{i})\cap\mathrm{SL}_{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{l}}(\mathbb{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i=1}^{l}\mathrm{GL}_{m_{i}}(\mathbb{C})\cap\mathrm{SL}_{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{l}}(\mathbb{C})}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle\sim

where the morphisms are the obvious ones. We impose this condition of having a determinant
1
/being trace-free because homotheties act trivially on Dolbeault operators.

4.1
Infinite dimensional moment map

The purpose of this article is to give necessary and sufficient conditions for small deformations of
\mathcal{E}_{0}
to have solutions to the
P
-critical equations in a neighbourhood of
\overline{\partial}_{0}
by using the moment map interpretation of this equation. We also make the parameter
\zeta
of the equation vary near
\zeta_{0}
. Recall that for all
\zeta
,

\mathcal{X}_{\zeta}=\{\overline{\partial}:\Omega^{0}(X,E)\rightarrow\Omega^{0,1}(X,E)|\overline{\partial}\textrm{ is a sub-solution to the $P_{\zeta}$-critical equation}\}.

And recall that
L_{d}^{p}(\mathcal{X}_{\zeta})
is the set of all
\overline{\partial}\in\overline{\partial}_{0}+L_{d}^{p}(\Omega^{0,1}(X,\mathrm{End}(E)))
which are sub-solutions to the
P_{\zeta}
-critical equation. Similarly with the
\mathcal{C}^{d}
and the
\mathcal{C}^{d+\alpha}
topologies. Since
\zeta
varies near
\zeta_{0}
, we need first to understand how it affects sub-solutions.

Lemma 4.1
.

If
(d-1)p>2n
, the inclusion

\bigsqcup_{\zeta\in L_{d-1}^{p}(\mathcal{Z})}(\zeta,L_{d}^{p}(\mathcal{X}_{\zeta}))\subset L_{d-1}^{p}(\mathcal{Z})\times(\overline{\partial}_{0}+L_{d}^{p}(\Omega^{0,1}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))))

is open. Similarly, if
d\geqslant 1
, the inclusions

\bigsqcup_{\zeta\in\mathcal{C}^{d-1}(\mathcal{Z})}(\zeta,\mathcal{C}^{d}(\mathcal{X}_{\zeta}))\subset\mathcal{C}^{d-1}(\mathcal{Z})\times(\overline{\partial}_{0}+\mathcal{C}^{d}(\Omega^{0,1}(X,\mathrm{End}(E)))),

\bigsqcup_{\zeta\in\mathcal{C}^{d-1,\alpha}(\mathcal{Z})}(\zeta,\mathcal{C}^{d,\alpha}(\mathcal{X}_{\zeta}))\subset\mathcal{C}^{d-1,\alpha}(\mathcal{Z})\times(\overline{\partial}_{0}+\mathcal{C}^{d,\alpha}(\Omega^{0,1}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))))

are open.

Proof.

For the first one, the inequality
(d-1)p>2n
implies that the wedge product

\wedge:\Omega^{p,q}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))\times\Omega^{p^{\prime},q^{\prime}}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))\rightarrow\Omega^{p+p^{\prime},q+q^{\prime}}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))

is continuous when these spaces are all endowed with the
L_{d-1}^{p}
topology. Moreover,
L_{d-1}^{p}(\mathcal{Z})\subset\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathcal{Z})
is continuous too. We deduce that
\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(\overline{\partial})
being positive is an open condition on
\zeta\in L_{d}^{p}(\mathcal{Z})
and
\overline{\partial}\in\overline{\partial}_{0}+L_{d}^{p}(\Omega^{0,1}(X,\mathrm{End}(E)))
. We deduce the first result.

For the
\mathcal{C}^{d}
and the
\mathcal{C}^{d,\alpha}
topologies, the argument is the same.
∎

From now on, let us fix
d\geqslant 1
. We shall only work with the
\mathcal{C}^{d}
topologies for simplicity but all our results also hold for
L_{d}^{p}
and
\mathcal{C}^{d,\alpha}
topologies as long as
(d-1)p>2n
(resp.
d\geqslant 1
) in order to satisfy Proposition
2.7
and Lemma
4.1
. The reason why we choose this topology is that it is the coarsest for
d=1
. Indeed, by Sobolev embedding theorems,
L_{d}^{p}\subset\mathcal{C}^{1}
if
(d-1)p>2n
and clearly
\mathcal{C}^{d,\alpha}\subset\mathcal{C}^{1,\alpha}\subset\mathcal{C}^{1}
.

By Lemma
4.1
and by definition of the product topology, there is an open neighbourhood
\mathcal{U}_{d}\subset\mathcal{C}^{d-1}(\mathcal{Z})
of
\zeta_{0}
and an open neighbourhood
\mathcal{X}_{d}
of
\overline{\partial}
in
\overline{\partial}_{0}+\mathcal{C}^{d}(\Omega^{0,1}(X,\mathrm{End}(E)))
such that

\mathcal{U}_{d}\times\mathcal{X}_{d}\subset\bigsqcup_{\zeta\in\mathcal{C}^{d-1}(\mathcal{Z})}(\zeta,\mathcal{C}^{d}(\mathcal{X}_{\zeta})).

In other words, for all
(\zeta,\overline{\partial})\in\mathcal{U}_{d}\times\mathcal{X}_{d}
,
\overline{\partial}
is a sub-solution to the
P
-critical equation with parameter
\zeta
. From now on, we only consider
\zeta\in\mathcal{U}_{d}
.

Let for all
\zeta
,

\mu_{\infty,\zeta}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{d}&\longrightarrow&\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{d-1}(\mathrm{Lie}(\mathcal{G}(E,h)))\\
\displaystyle\overline{\partial}&\longmapsto&\displaystyle-\frac{2\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\pi}{\mathrm{Vol}}\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(\overline{\partial})\end{array}\right..

Notice that
\mathrm{Lie}(\mathcal{G}(E,h))=\Omega^{0}(X,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\mathrm{End}_{H}(E,h))
where
\mathrm{End}_{H}(E,h)
denotes the real vector bundle of Hermitian endomorphisms of
E
(for the metric
h
), so
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\mathrm{End}_{H}(E,h)
is the bundle of skew-Hermitian endomorphisms of
E
.

By Propositions
2.6
and
2.7
,
\mu_{\infty,\zeta}
is smooth on each germ of
\mathcal{C}^{d+1}(\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}(E))
-orbit and for all
\overline{\partial}\in\mathcal{X}_{d}
,

\frac{d}{ds}|_{s=0}\mu_{\infty,\zeta}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{s}\cdot\overline{\partial}):v\mapsto-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\Delta_{\zeta,\nabla}\Re(v)+2\pi\left[\Im(v),\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(\overline{\partial})\right].

The problem of finding
P
-critical connections now reduces to finding zeroes of
\mu_{\infty,\zeta}
.

In Sub-section
2.2
, we introduced
L^{2}
Hermitian products on
\Omega^{0,1}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))
depending on parameters
\zeta
and sub-solutions
\overline{\partial}
. Let
