\Omega_{\infty,\zeta}
be the associated Kähler form,

\Omega_{\infty,\zeta,\overline{\partial}}(\alpha,\beta)=\Im(\left<\alpha,\beta\right>_{L^{2}(\overline{\partial},\zeta)})=\int_{X}\Re\left(\mathrm{tr}([\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(\overline{\partial})\wedge\alpha\wedge\beta^{\dagger}]_{\mathrm{sym}}))\right).

Using the same arguments as in the proof of Proposition
2.7
, the map

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{d}\times\mathcal{X}_{d}&\longrightarrow&\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}(\mathcal{C}^{d}(\Omega^{0,1}(X,\mathrm{End}(E)))\otimes\mathcal{C}^{d}(\Omega^{0,1}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))),\mathcal{C}^{d-1}(\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))))\\
\displaystyle(\zeta,\overline{\partial})&\longmapsto&\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\prime}(\overline{\partial})\end{array}\right.

is well-defined ans smooth. Moreover, it is
\mathcal{C}^{d+1}(\mathcal{G}(E,h))
-invariant. We deduce that
\Omega_{\infty,\zeta}
extends as a Kähler form on
\mathcal{X}_{d}
and it varies smoothly with
\zeta\in\mathcal{C}^{d-1}(\mathcal{Z})
. However, it does not make it locally complete because the induced norm is an
L^{2}
norm. The following Proposition comes from
[
18
, Theorem 2.45]
. See also
[
17
, Theorem 6.7]
for reference.

Proposition 4.2
.

The (germ of) action of
\mathcal{C}^{d+1}(\mathcal{G}(E,h))
on
(\mathcal{X}_{d},\Omega_{\infty,\zeta})
is Hamiltonian with associated
\mathcal{C}^{d+1}(\mathcal{G}(E,h))
-equivariant moment map
\mu_{\infty,\zeta}
.

Remark
.

We could have used Dervan–Hallam theory
[
17
, Theorem 6.7]
directly instead of constructing
\Omega_{\infty,\zeta}
but it wouldn’t have given us to continuity with respect to
\zeta
with the involved topologies. We use here the method of
[
18
]
to define
\Omega_{\infty,\zeta}
thanks to the formal derivative of
\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}
. It has the advantage of giving a more explicit definition of this Kähler form and to grant us the wanted continuity and smoothness results.

4.2
Kuranishi slices and finite dimensional moment maps

An important tool in deformation theory is the Kuranishi slice, which is basically a way to parametrise all complex deformations of a complex object (here, a holomorphic vector bundle) by a finite dimensional analytic space. It can be found in various forms in literature
[
30
]
,
[
29
, Section 7.3]
,
[
5
, Theorem 1]
. We give here a small informal exposition.

The gauge group
\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}(E)
acts on the space of
(0,1)
-forms with values in
\mathrm{End}(E)
by conjugation,

f\cdot\alpha=f\alpha f^{-1}.

The closed positive
(n-1,n-1)
-form
\frac{\omega^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}
on
X
defines an
L^{2}
norm on the space of
(0,1)
-forms with values in the endomorphisms of
E
, on which
\mathcal{G}(E,h)
acts by unitary actions,

\left\|\alpha\right\|_{L^{2}}=\int_{X}\mathrm{tr}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\alpha^{\dagger}\wedge\alpha)\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}.

Let
\Lambda
be the contraction with respect to
\omega
on
(1,1)
-forms,

\Lambda\alpha=\frac{\alpha\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}}{\frac{\omega^{n}}{n!}}.

For this norm, the adjoint of the
\overline{\partial}_{0}
(still on
\mathrm{End}(E)
) operator is

\overline{\partial}_{0}^{\bullet}=-\star\partial_{0}\star,

where
\star
is the Hodge star. We use the notation
\bullet
instead of
*
to avoid confusion with the adjoints of
\overline{\partial}_{0}
with respect to the
\left<\cdot,\cdot\right>_{L^{2}(\overline{\partial}_{0},\zeta)}
introduced in Sub-section
2.2
. Moreover, on the space of sections,

\overline{\partial}_{0}^{\bullet}=-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\Lambda\partial_{0}\textrm{ on }\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E)).

It is a well-known fact on Kähler manifolds and it remains true for balanced manifolds at degree
0
. See
[
14
, Lemma 2.1]
or Lemma
2.5
in the balanced HYM case.

Let
V=H^{0,1}(X,\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{E}))
be the space of harmonic
(0,1)
-forms
i.e.
the space of

b\in\Omega^{0,1}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))
such that
\overline{\partial}_{0}b=\overline{\partial}_{0}^{\bullet}b=0
. It is preserved by the action of
G\subset\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}(E)
. We endow it with the induced
L^{2}
norm so
G
acts on
V
and
K
acts on
V
by unitary actions.

Hodge theory tells us that
V
is finite dimensional and we have an
L^{2}
orthogonal decomposition
[
16
, Theorem 7.1]
,

\Omega^{0,1}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))=\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\overbrace{\phantom{\mathrm{im}(\overline{\partial}_{0})\oplus V}}^{=\ker(\overline{\partial}_{0})}$\hss}\mathrm{im}(\overline{\partial}_{0})\oplus\underbrace{V\oplus\mathrm{im}(\overline{\partial}_{0}^{\bullet})}_{=\ker(\overline{\partial}_{0}^{\bullet})}.

(5)

When we apply a gauge transform of the form
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{s}
to
\overline{\partial}_{0}
, we obtain
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{s}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{0}=\overline{\partial}_{0}+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{s}\overline{\partial}_{0}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-s})
, which is close to
\overline{\partial}_{0}-\overline{\partial}_{0}s
when
s
is close to
0
. Therefore, the direction tangent to the gauge orbit of
\overline{\partial}_{0}
at
\overline{\partial}_{0}
is
\mathrm{im}(\overline{\partial}_{0})
.

Consequently, the small deformations of
\overline{\partial}_{0}
can be chosen in the orthogonal direction
i.e.

\ker(\overline{\partial}_{0}^{\bullet})
. Moreover,
\overline{\partial}=\overline{\partial}_{0}+\gamma
is integrable if and only if
\overline{\partial}_{0}\gamma+\gamma\wedge\gamma=0
. We see that in this case, if
\gamma
is small,
\overline{\partial}_{0}\gamma=-\gamma\wedge\gamma
is small with quadratic order in
\gamma
. We deduce that small integrable deformations of
\overline{\partial}_{0}
can be chosen in the direction of
\ker(\overline{\partial}_{0})\cap\ker(\overline{\partial}_{0}^{\bullet})=V
, which is finite dimensional.

The
Kuranishi slice
is the rigorous statement of this observation.

Proposition 4.3
.

There is and open ball
B\subset V
centred at
0
and a holomorphic embedding
\Phi:(B,0)\rightarrow(\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\Omega^{0,1}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))),0)
such that,

1.

\Phi
takes values in
\ker(\overline{\partial}_{0}^{\bullet})
.

2.

\Phi
is
G
invariant i.e. for all
b\in B
and all
g\in G
such that
g\cdot b\in B
,
\Phi(g\cdot b)=g\cdot\Phi(b)
.

3.

For all
b
,
b=\Phi(b)+\overline{\partial}_{0}^{\bullet}\mathrm{Green}(\Phi(b)\wedge\Phi(b))
where
\mathrm{Green}
is the Green operator of the Laplace–Beltrami operator
\overline{\partial}_{0}\overline{\partial}_{0}^{\bullet}+\overline{\partial}_{0}^{\bullet}\overline{\partial}_{0}
. In particular,
d\Phi(0):v\mapsto v
.

4.

For all
\overline{\partial}
which is
\mathcal{C}^{d}
close enough to
\overline{\partial}_{0}
and integrable, there is a unique
s\in\ker(\overline{\partial}_{0})^{\bot}
whose
\mathcal{C}^{d+1}
norm is bounded by a multiple of the
\mathcal{C}^{d}
norm of
\overline{\partial}-\overline{\partial}_{0}
such that
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{s}\cdot\overline{\partial}\in\Phi(B)
.

5.

The set of all
b\in B
such that
\overline{\partial}_{0}+\Phi(b)
is integrable is an analytic subset of
B
whose equation is given by
\Pi(\Phi(b)\wedge\Phi(b))=0
, where
\Pi:\Omega^{0,2}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))\rightarrow H^{0,2}(X,\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{E}_{0}))
is the
L^{2}
orthogonal projection onto the space of harmonic
(0,2)
-forms.

6.

The map

G\cdot b\mapsto\mathcal{C}^{d+1}(\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}(E))\cdot\Phi(b)

is a well-defined and continuous. Moreover, it induces a local homeomorphism from the space of
b
modulo
G
such that
\overline{\partial}_{0}+\Phi(b)
is integrable to the space of integrable
\mathcal{C}^{d}
Dolbeault operators modulo
\mathcal{C}^{d+1}(\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}(E))
.

Moreover, by openness of
\mathcal{X}_{d}
, we may assume, up to shrinking
B
, that
\Phi(B)\subset\mathcal{X}_{d}
so the closed
(1,1)
-forms

\Omega_{\zeta}=\Phi^{*}\Omega_{\infty,\zeta}

are well-defined and positive on the whole
B
. They define Kähler metrics which vary smoothly with
\zeta
. From now on, when
b\in B
, we set

\overline{\partial}_{b}=\overline{\partial}_{0}+\Phi(b)\in\mathcal{X}_{d}.

Let
\Pi_{\mathfrak{k}}:\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))\rightarrow\mathfrak{k}
be the
L^{2}
orthogonal projection on
\mathfrak{k}
and

\mu_{\zeta}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle B&\longrightarrow&\displaystyle\mathfrak{k}\\
\displaystyle b&\longmapsto&\displaystyle\Pi_{\mathfrak{k}}\mu_{\infty,\zeta}(\overline{\partial}_{b})\end{array}\right..

Then,
\mu
is a smooth map between finite dimensional spaces by composition and it can also be interpreted as a moment map. It varies smoothly with
\zeta
too.

Proposition 4.4
.

The action of
K
on
(B,\Omega_{\zeta})
is Hamiltonian with associated
K
-equivariant moment map
\mu_{\zeta}
.

Proof.

It is a direct consequence of Proposition
4.2
. See also
[
17
, Theorem 6.8]
.
∎

This moment map is defined between finite dimensional vector spaces so we will be able to use classical GIT results
[
18
]
. The fact that
B
is non compact will be compensated by the fact that we are only looking for local results around
0
. Therefore, finding which
G
-orbits contain zeroes of
\mu_{\zeta}
will be easier than finding which
\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}(E)
-orbits contain zeroes of
\mu_{\infty,\zeta}
. However, the zeroes
b
of
\mu_{\zeta}
only give operators
\overline{\partial}_{b}
on
E
such that
\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(\overline{\partial}_{b})\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\mathfrak{k}^{\bot}\mathrm{Vol}
, which is weaker than being
0
.

Nevertheless, our main theorem (Theorem
4.12
) will ensure that finding zeroes of
\mu_{\zeta}
is more or less equivalent to finding zeroes of
\mu_{\infty,\zeta}
so the complicated infinite dimensional problem can be reduced to a more simple finite dimensional one.

In order to prove Theorem
4.12
, we still need of course to be able to find zeroes of
\mu_{\infty,\zeta}
. For this, we introduce an other finite dimensional moment map whose zeroes will correspond to actual zeroes of
\mu_{\infty,\zeta}
. The following Proposition is analogous to
[
9
, Proposition 3.2]
,
[
14
, Proposition 4.1]
.

Proposition 4.5
.

Up to shrinking
B
and
\mathcal{U}_{d}
, there exists a smooth map

\sigma:\mathcal{U}_{d}\times B\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{d+1}(\Omega^{0}_{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))),

that verifies
\sigma(\zeta_{0},0)=0
,
\frac{\partial}{\partial b}_{|b=0}\sigma(\zeta_{0},b)=0
, all
\sigma(\zeta,b)
are Hermitian and orthogonal to
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\mathfrak{k}
and the following maps are well-defined and smooth.

\tilde{\Phi}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{d}\times B&\longrightarrow&\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{d}-\overline{\partial}_{0}\\
\displaystyle(\zeta,b)&\longmapsto&\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\sigma(\zeta,b)}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{b}-\overline{\partial}_{0}\end{array}\right.,\qquad\tilde{\mu}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{d}\times B&\longrightarrow&\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{d-1}(\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}(E)))\\
\displaystyle(\zeta,b)&\longmapsto&\displaystyle\mu_{\infty,\zeta}(\overline{\partial}_{0}+\tilde{\Phi}(b))\end{array}\right..

Moreover, they verify,

1.

For all
\zeta
and
b
,
\overline{\partial}_{\zeta,b}=\overline{\partial}_{0}+\tilde{\Phi}(\zeta,b)
is gauge equivalent to
\overline{\partial}_{b}
.

2.

For all
\zeta
,
b\mapsto\overline{\partial}_{\zeta,b}
is
K
-equivariant.

3.

For all
\zeta
and
b
,
\tilde{\mu}_{\zeta}(b)\in\mathfrak{k}
. Reciprocally, if
\overline{\partial}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{s}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{b}
verifies
\mu_{\infty,\zeta}(\overline{\partial})\in\mathfrak{k}
,
b\in B
and
s\in\mathcal{C}^{d+1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\mathfrak{k}^{\bot})
is small enough, then
s=\sigma(\zeta,b)
, so
\overline{\partial}=\overline{\partial}_{\zeta,b}
.

4.

\frac{\partial}{\partial b}_{|b=0}\tilde{\Phi}(\zeta_{0},b):v\mapsto v
. In particular, up to shrinking
B
and
\mathcal{U}_{d}
, each
\tilde{\Phi}(\zeta,\cdot)
is a smooth embedding.

Proof.

Let
\Pi_{\mathfrak{k}^{\bot}}:\Omega^{0}(X,\mathrm{End}_{H}(E,h))\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\mathfrak{k}^{\bot}
be the orthogonal projection onto
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\mathfrak{k}^{\bot}
with respect
\left<\cdot,\cdot\right>_{L^{2}}
. Notice that we write abusively
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\mathfrak{k}^{\bot}
to denote Hermitian global sections of
E
which are orthogonal to
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\mathfrak{k}
. Let

\Psi:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{d}\times B\times\mathcal{C}^{d+1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\mathfrak{k}^{\bot})&\longrightarrow&\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{d-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\mathfrak{k}^{\bot})\\
\displaystyle(\zeta,b,s)&\longmapsto&\displaystyle\Pi_{\mathfrak{k}^{\bot}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\mu_{\infty,\zeta}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{s}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{b})\end{array}\right..

With the same arguments as in the proof of Proposition
2.7
, we see that
\Psi
is smooth and by Proposition
2.6
,
\frac{\partial}{\partial s}_{|s=0}\Psi(\zeta_{0},0,s)=\Pi_{\mathfrak{k}^{\bot}}\Delta_{\zeta_{0},\nabla_{0}}
.
\Delta_{\zeta_{0},\nabla_{0}}=\nabla_{0,\zeta_{0}}^{*}\nabla_{0}
is symmetric positive so
\mathrm{coker}(\Delta_{\zeta_{0},\nabla_{0}})=\ker(\Delta_{\zeta_{0},\nabla_{0}})=\ker(\nabla_{0})=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\mathfrak{k}
by Lemma
2.8
. We deduce that
\frac{\partial}{\partial s}_{|s=0}\Psi(\zeta_{0},0,s)=\Delta_{\zeta_{0},\nabla_{0}}:\mathcal{C}^{d+1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\mathfrak{k}^{\bot})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{d-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\mathfrak{k}^{\bot})
is an isomorphism by Proposition
2.7
.

By the implicit functions theorem, up to shrinking
B
and
\mathcal{U}_{d}
, there is a unique smooth
\sigma:\mathcal{U}_{d}\times B\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{d+1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\mathfrak{k}^{\bot})
such that
\sigma(\zeta_{0},0)=0
and for all
\zeta
and
b
,
\Psi(\zeta,b,\sigma(\zeta,b))=0
. When we set
\tilde{\Phi}(\zeta,b)=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\sigma(\zeta,b)}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{b}-\overline{\partial}_{0}
, the first point is immediate and the third point is verified. For the second one, notice that for all
u\in K
and all
\zeta
and
b
,

\displaystyle\Psi(\zeta,b,u^{\dagger}\sigma(\zeta,u\cdot b)u)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\mathfrak{k}^{\bot}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\mu_{\infty,\zeta}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{u^{\dagger}\sigma(\zeta,u\cdot b)u}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{b})

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\mathfrak{k}^{\bot}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\mu_{\infty,\zeta}(u^{\dagger}\cdot(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\sigma(\zeta,u\cdot b)}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{u\cdot b}))

\displaystyle=u^{\dagger}\Pi_{\mathfrak{k}^{\bot}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\mu_{\infty,\zeta}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\sigma(\zeta,u\cdot b)}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{u\cdot b})u

\displaystyle=u^{\dagger}\Psi(\zeta,u\cdot b,\sigma(\zeta,u\cdot b))u

\displaystyle=0.

By uniqueness of
\sigma
, it implies that
u^{\dagger}\sigma(\zeta,u\cdot b)u=\sigma(\zeta,b)
. Therefore,

\overline{\partial}_{\zeta,u\cdot b}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\sigma(\zeta,u\cdot b)}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{u\cdot b}=u\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\sigma(\zeta,b)}u^{\dagger}u\overline{\partial}_{b}u^{\dagger}=u\cdot\overline{\partial}_{\zeta,b}.

It proves the second point. For the fourth point, we have for all
b
close to
0
,

\displaystyle\Psi(\zeta_{0},b,0)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\mathfrak{k}^{\bot}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\mu_{\infty,\zeta_{0}}(\overline{\partial}_{0}+\Phi(b))

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\mathfrak{k}^{\bot}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\nabla_{0,\zeta_{0}}^{*}(\Phi(b)-\Phi(b)^{\dagger})+\mathrm{o}(\Phi(b))

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\mathfrak{k}^{\bot}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\nabla_{0,\zeta_{0}}^{*}(d\Phi(0)b-(d\Phi(0)b)^{\dagger})+\mathrm{o}(b).

Therefore,
\frac{\partial}{\partial b}_{|b=0}\Psi(\zeta_{0},b,0)v=\Pi_{\mathfrak{k}^{\bot}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\nabla_{0,\zeta_{0}}^{*}(d\Phi(0)v-(d\Phi(0)v)^{\dagger})
. Now, recall that the image of
d\Phi(0):v\mapsto v
is the space
V
of
(0,1)
-harmonic forms. In particular, it is included in the kernel of
\nabla_{0,\zeta_{0}}^{*}
. Similarly for
(d\Phi(0)\cdot)^{\dagger}
. Thus
\frac{\partial}{\partial b}_{|b=0}\Psi(\zeta_{0},b,0)=0
. Therefore, when we differentiate the equality
\Psi(\zeta_{0},b,\sigma(\zeta_{0},b))
at
b=0
,

0=\frac{\partial}{\partial b}|_{b=0}\Psi(\zeta_{0},b,0)+\frac{\partial}{\partial s}|_{s=0}\Psi(\zeta_{0},0,s)\frac{\partial}{\partial b}_{|b=0}\sigma(\zeta_{0},b)=\frac{\partial}{\partial s}|_{s=0}\Psi(\zeta_{0},0,s)\frac{\partial}{\partial b}_{|b=0}\sigma(\zeta_{0},b).

Since
\frac{\partial}{\partial s}|_{s=0}\Psi(\zeta_{0},0,s)
is an isomorphism, we have
\frac{\partial}{\partial b}_{|b=0}\sigma(\zeta_{0},b)=0
hence

\tilde{\Phi}(\zeta_{0},b)=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\sigma(\zeta_{0},b)}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{b}-\overline{\partial}_{0}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\sigma(\zeta_{0},b)}\overline{\partial}_{0}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-\sigma(\zeta_{0},b)})+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\sigma(\zeta_{0},b)}\Phi(b)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-\sigma(\zeta_{0},b)}=\Phi(b)+\mathrm{o}(b)=b+\mathrm{o}(b).

We deduce the fourth point and we showed along the way that
\frac{\partial}{\partial b}_{|b=0}\sigma(\zeta_{0},b)=0
. Finally, all the
\sigma(\zeta_{0},b)
take values in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\mathfrak{k}^{\bot}
by definition of
\sigma
.
∎

Similarly as the
\mu_{\zeta}
, the
\tilde{\mu}_{\zeta}
are moment maps on
B
. Let
\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta}=\tilde{\Phi}(\zeta,\cdot)^{*}\Omega_{\infty,\zeta}
which is a close symplectic form on
B
.

Proposition 4.6
.

The action of
K
on
(B,\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta})
is Hamiltonian with associated
K
-equivariant moment map
\tilde{\mu}_{\zeta}
.

Proof.

It is a direct consequence of Propositions
4.2
and
4.5
.
∎

Remark
.

The fact that
\tilde{\Phi}(\zeta,\cdot)
is only smooth (and not holomorphic) means that
\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta}
is not compatible with the complex structure of
B
. However, as a small deformation of
\Omega_{\zeta_{0}}
, the form

(v,w)\mapsto-\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}v,w)

will be positive (see Lemma
4.7
). However, the incompatibility of
\tilde{\Phi}(\zeta,\cdot)
with the complex structure of
B
implies that the above bilinear form need not be symmetric.

Lemma 4.7
.

Up to shrinking
B
and
\mathcal{U}_{d}
, there is a positive constant
c
such that for all
b\in B
and
v\in T_{b}B=V
,

-\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta,b}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}v,v)\geqslant c\left\|v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.5
, we have
\frac{\partial}{\partial b}_{|b=0}\tilde{\Phi}(\zeta_{0},b):v\mapsto v
. Thus, at
\zeta=\zeta_{0}
and
b=0
,

-\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta_{0},0}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}v,v)=-\tilde{\Omega}_{\infty,\zeta_{0},0}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}v,v)=\left\|v\right\|_{L^{2}(\overline{\partial}_{0},\zeta_{0})}^{2}\geqslant 2c\left\|v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

for some
c>0
. We deduce the wanted equality by continuity of
\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta,b}
in
\zeta
and
b
.
∎

4.3
Statement of the main theorem

Let us define some different notions of stability, all related to stability in GIT, that we will show to be equivalent.

Definition 4.8

(
[
24
, Definition 7.1]
)
.

A point
b\in B
is said to be
\mu_{\zeta}
-semi-stable
in
B
if
\mu_{\zeta}
vanishes on the closure
\overline{G\cdot b}\cap B
of the orbit of
b
.

It is said to be
\mu_{\zeta}
-polystable
in
B
if
\mu_{\zeta}
vanishes in the orbit
G\cdot b\cap B
of
b
.

It is said to be
\mu_{\zeta}
-stable
in
B
if it is
\mu_{\zeta}
-polystable and the stabiliser
\mathrm{Stab}(b)\subset G
of
b
is trivial.

Remark
.

Usually, in GIT, a point is stable when it is polystable and has a
discrete
stabiliser. Here, we will see that the stabiliser of a point corresponds to the group of holomorphic automorphisms of a vector bundle. These are always connected so in this context, discrete is equivalent to trivial.

Similarly, we introduce
\mu_{\infty,\zeta}
-(semi-)(poly)stability of a vector bundle
\mathcal{E}_{b}=(E,\overline{\partial}_{b})
whose Dolbeault operator is in the Kuranishi slice.

Definition 4.9
.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{d}
be an open neighbourhood of
\overline{\partial}_{0}
in
\mathcal{X}_{d}
and
b\in B
.

\mathcal{E}_{b}
is said to be
\mu_{\infty,\zeta}
-semi-stable
in
\mathcal{B}_{d}
if
\mu_{\infty,\zeta}
vanishes on
\overline{\mathcal{C}^{d+1}(\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}(E))\cdot\overline{\partial}_{b}}\cap\mathcal{B}_{d}
.

It is said to be
\mu_{\infty,\zeta}
-polystable
in
\mathcal{B}_{d}
if
\mu_{\infty,\zeta}
vanishes on
\mathcal{C}^{d+1}(\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}(E))\cdot\overline{\partial}_{b}\cap\mathcal{B}_{d}
.

It is said to be
\mu_{\infty,\zeta}
-stable
if it is
\mu_{\infty,\zeta}
-polystable and if
\mathcal{E}_{b}
is simple.

Finally, we introduce local
P
-stability of a bundle
\mathcal{E}_{b}=(E,\overline{\partial}_{b})
which basically says that
\mathcal{E}
does not have sub-bundles which are deformations of direct sums of the simple components of
\mathcal{E}_{0}
.

Definition 4.10
.

We say that a smooth sub-bundle
F
of
E
is an
admissible
sub-bundle of
\mathcal{E}_{b}
if
(F,\overline{\partial}_{b})\subset\mathcal{E}_{b}
,
(F,\overline{\partial}_{0})\subset\mathcal{E}_{0}
and
(F^{\bot},\overline{\partial}_{0})\subset\mathcal{E}_{0}
are holomorphic sub-bundles.

In particular, if
F
is admissible,
(F,\overline{\partial}_{0})
is isomorphic to a direct sum of
\mathcal{G}_{0,i}
s (the simple components of
\mathcal{E}_{0}
). It implies that there is a finite number of them up to isomorphism (with respect to
\overline{\partial}_{0}
).

Definition 4.11
.

Let
0\subsetneq F\subset E
an admissible sub-bundle of
\mathcal{E}_{b}
.

(F,\overline{\partial}_{b})
is said to be
locally
P_{\zeta}
-semi-stable
if for all
0\subsetneq G\subsetneq F
which are admissible in
\mathcal{E}_{b}
,
\frac{P_{\zeta}(G)}{\mathrm{rk}(G)}\leqslant\frac{P_{\zeta}(F)}{\mathrm{rk}(F)}
.

It is said to be
locally
P_{\zeta}
-polystable
if for all
0\subsetneq G\subsetneq F
which are admissible in
\mathcal{E}_{b}
,
\frac{P_{\zeta}(G)}{\mathrm{rk}(G)}\leqslant\frac{P_{\zeta}(F)}{\mathrm{rk}(F)}
and in case of equality, the inclusion
(G^{\bot},\overline{\partial}_{b})\subset(F,\overline{\partial}_{b})
is holomorphic.

It is said to be
locally
P_{\zeta}
-stable
if for all
0\subsetneq G\subsetneq F
which are admissible in
\mathcal{E}_{b}
,
\frac{P_{\zeta}(G)}{\mathrm{rk}(G)}<\frac{P_{\zeta}(F)}{\mathrm{rk}(F)}
.

By an immediate induction, we see that any locally
P_{\zeta}
-polystable
(F,\overline{\partial}_{b})\subset\mathcal{E}_{b}
can be decomposed as a direct sum of locally
P_{\zeta}
-stable simple admissible components
(G_{i},\overline{\partial}_{b})
verifying
\frac{P_{\zeta}(G_{i})}{\mathrm{rk}(G_{i})}=\frac{P_{\zeta}(F)}{\mathrm{rk}(F)}
. Notice also that
\mathcal{E}_{0}
is locally
P_{\zeta_{0}}
-polystable.

We can now state our main theorems.

Theorem 4.12

(Local Kobayashi–Hitchin correspondence)
.

There is an open neighbourhood
\mathcal{B}_{d}
of
\overline{\partial}_{0}
in
\mathcal{X}_{d}
and an open neighbourhood
\mathcal{U}_{d}
of
\zeta_{0}
in
\mathcal{C}^{d-1}(\mathcal{Z})
such that for all small enough open ball
B_{2}\subset V
of centre
0
, there is an open ball
B_{1}\subset B_{2}
of centre
0
such that for all
\zeta\in\mathcal{U}_{d}
and
b\in B_{1}
, we have equivalence between,

1.

b
is
\mu_{\zeta}
-semi-stable (resp. polystable, resp. stable) in
B_{2}
,

2.

\mathcal{E}_{b}
is
\mu_{\infty,\zeta}
-semi-stable (resp. polystable, resp. stable) in
\mathcal{B}_{d}
,

3.

\mathcal{E}_{b}
is locally
P_{\zeta}
-semi-stable (resp. polystable, resp. stable).

Moreover, at
\zeta=\zeta_{0}
(when we don’t change the parameter of the equation), we have,

Theorem 4.13
.

With the same
B_{2}
and
B_{1}
as in Theorem
4.12
, we have, for all
b\in B_{1}
,

1.

b
is
\mu_{\zeta_{0}}
-semi-stable in
B_{2}
.

2.

b
is
\mu_{\zeta_{0}}
-polystable in
B_{2}
if and only if
G\cdot b
is closed in
V
.

Then, we give a topological description of sets of couples
(\zeta,b)
making
\mathcal{E}_{b}

\mu_{\infty,\zeta}
-semi-stable, polystable and stable. Let

L_{\mathrm{ss}}=\{(\zeta,b)\in\mathcal{U}_{d}\times B_{1}|b\textrm{ is $\mu_{\zeta}$-semi-stable in }B_{2}\},

L_{\mathrm{ps}}=\{(\zeta,b)\in\mathcal{U}_{d}\times B_{1}|\mathcal{E}_{b}\textrm{ is $\mu_{\zeta}$-polystable in }B_{2}\},

L_{\mathrm{s}}=\{(\zeta,b)\in\mathcal{U}_{d}\times B_{1}|\mathcal{E}_{b}\textrm{ is $\mu_{\zeta}$-stable in }B_{2}\}.

Clearly,
L_{\mathrm{s}}\subset L_{\mathrm{ps}}\subset L_{\mathrm{ss}}\subset\mathcal{U}_{d}\times B_{1}
.

Proposition 4.14
.

For all
(\zeta,b)\in\mathcal{U}_{d}\times B_{1}
,
(\zeta,b)
belonging to
L_{\mathrm{s}}
(resp.
L_{\mathrm{ps}}
,
L_{\mathrm{ss}}
) only depends on
[\zeta]
and
G\cdot b\cap B_{1}
. Moreover,

1.

L_{\mathrm{s}}\subset\mathcal{U}_{d}\times B_{1}
is open.

2.

For all fixed
\zeta\in\mathcal{U}_{d}
,
\{b\in B_{1}|(\zeta,b)\in L_{\mathrm{ss}}\}\subset B_{1}
is open.

3.

For all fixed
b\in B_{1}
,
\{\zeta\in\mathcal{U}_{d}|(\zeta,b)\in L_{\mathrm{ss}}\}\subset\mathcal{U}_{d}
is a closed polyhedral cone centred at
0
defined by a finite number of inequalities on
[\zeta-\zeta_{0}]
and
\{\zeta\in\mathcal{U}_{d}|(\zeta,b)\in L_{\mathrm{s}}\}\subset\mathcal{U}_{d}
is its interior.

4.

For all fixed
b\in B_{1}
, if
(\zeta_{0},b)\in L_{\mathrm{ps}}
,
\{\zeta\in\mathcal{U}_{d}|(\zeta,b)\in L_{\mathrm{ps}}\}=\{\zeta\in\mathcal{U}_{d}|(\zeta,b)\in L_{\mathrm{ss}}\}
.

5.

For all fixed
b\in B_{1}
, if
\mathcal{E}_{b}
is simple,
\{\zeta\in\mathcal{U}_{d}|(\zeta,b)\in L_{\mathrm{ps}}\}=\{\zeta\in\mathcal{U}_{d}|(\zeta,b)\in L_{\mathrm{s}}\}
.

In Section
5
, we show some local GIT results in a local non-compact symplectic setting and we fix
\mathcal{B}_{d}
. We also study some properties of the moment map flows including their global existence and convergence at
\zeta=\zeta_{0}
. This section gives all the tools to complete the proofs of the main theorems of the paper.

In the first half of Section
6
, we prove Theorem
4.12
under an hypothesis of global existence of the flows. In the second half, we use the existence of the moment map flows at all time when
\zeta=\zeta_{0}
and a version of the second Ness uniqueness theorem in the semi-stable case to deduce that the flows exist and converge at all time and for all
\zeta
. It will conclude the proof of Theorem
4.12
. Proofs of Theorem
4.13
and Proposition
4.14
will easily follow. We also construct along the way Jordan–Hölder filtrations of locally
P_{\zeta}
-semi-stable bundles by admissible sub-bundles (Theorem
6.3
) and we show the uniqueness of solutions of the
P_{\zeta}
-critical equation in
\mathcal{B}_{d}
modulo the unitary gauge group, under existence assumption (Theorem
6.2
).

In Section
7
, we construct Harder–Narasimhan filtrations of any small deformation of
\mathcal{E}_{0}
by admissible sub-bundles (Theorem
7.1
), we show a continuity result about the solutions of the
P_{\zeta}
-critical equations (Theorem
7.2
) and we deduce a local version of the Kempf–Ness theorem (Theorem
7.4
).

5
Local GIT results

5.1
Degeneration by
1
-parameter sub-groups

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{F}=(F,h,\overline{\partial}_{F})
be a holomorphic Hermitian vector bundle (so
\overline{\partial}_{F}
is integrable) such that all global holomorphic sections of
\mathcal{F}
are parallel. For all
\gamma\in\Omega^{0,1}(X,\mathrm{End}(F))
whose
\mathcal{C}^{0}
norm is small enough (depending on
\mathcal{F}
) such that
\overline{\partial}_{F}^{\bullet}\gamma=0
, all
(\overline{\partial}_{F}+\gamma)
-holomorphic global sections of
F
are
\overline{\partial}_{F}
-holomorphic.

Proof.

The proof is the same as the proof of
[
5
, Proposition 4.5]
. It uses the fact that the Kähler identity
\overline{\partial}_{F}^{\bullet}=-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\Lambda\partial_{F}
on smooth sections remains true on balanced manifolds.
∎

Corollary 5.2
.

Up to shrinking
B
, for all
(b,b^{\prime})\in B^{2}
, any holomorphic morphism
\xi:\mathcal{E}_{b}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{b^{\prime}}
lies in
\mathfrak{g}
. In particular,
\mathcal{E}_{b}\cong\mathcal{E}_{b^{\prime}}
is and only if
b\in G\cdot b^{\prime}
and any holomorphic endomorphism of
\mathcal{E}_{b}
is a holomorphic endomorphism of
\mathcal{E}_{0}
.

Proof.

This is a consequence of the continuity of
\Phi
, the fact that it takes values in
\ker(\overline{\partial}_{0}^{\bullet})
(see Proposition
4.3
), Lemma
2.8
and Lemma
5.1
applied to
\mathcal{F}=\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{E}_{0})
.
∎

To complete the proof of Theorem
4.12
, we need to construct four open balls centred at
0
with certain properties. We set
B_{4,0}\subset V
such a ball and we choose it small enough to satisfy all the previous results. We still call
B
a ball smaller than
B_{4,0}
and we allow ourselves to shrink it later. However,
B_{4,0}
is fixed until the end of this paper.

We now give a link between the existence of the limit
\lim_{t\rightarrow+\infty}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t\xi}\cdot b
for
b\in B
close enough to
0
and
\xi\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\mathfrak{k}
in terms of holomorphic sub-bundles of
\mathcal{E}_{b}
. For all
\xi\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\mathfrak{k}
,
\xi
is Hermitian and the coefficients of its characteristic polynomial are holomorphic thus constant. We deduce that the eigenvalues of
\xi
are constant so we can write, by the spectral theorem,

\xi=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathrm{Sp}(\xi)}\lambda\Pi_{\lambda},

where the
\Pi_{\lambda}\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\mathfrak{k}
are the orthogonal projection onto the eigenspaces of
\xi
. These eigenspaces define pairwise orthogonal smooth sub-bundles
G_{\xi,\lambda}
of
E
such that
(G_{\xi,\lambda},\overline{\partial}_{0})\subset\mathcal{E}_{0}
and
(G_{\xi,\lambda}^{\bot},\overline{\partial}_{0})\subset\mathcal{E}_{0}
are holomorphic sub-bundles.

We also define

F_{\xi,\lambda}=\bigoplus_{\nu\leqslant\lambda}G_{\xi,\nu}.

These bundles also verify
(F_{\xi,\lambda},\overline{\partial}_{0})\subset\mathcal{E}_{0}
and
(F_{\xi,\lambda}^{\bot},\overline{\partial}_{0})\subset\mathcal{E}_{0}
being holomorphic, and they define a filtration of
\mathcal{E}_{0}
by holomorphic sub-bundles.

Proposition 5.3
.

Up to shrinking
B
and
\mathcal{U}_{d}
, for all
\zeta\in\mathcal{U}_{d}
, all
b\in B
and all
\xi=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathrm{Sp}(\xi)}\lambda\Pi_{\lambda}
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\mathfrak{k}
, we have equivalence between,

1.

For all
\lambda
,
(F_{\xi,\lambda},\overline{\partial}_{b})\subset\mathcal{E}_{b}
is holomorphic (in other words, they define a filtration of
\mathcal{E}_{b}
by admissible sub-bundles),

2.

For all
t\geqslant 0
,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t\xi}\cdot b\in B_{4,0}
and it converges in
B_{4,0}
when
t\rightarrow+\infty
,

3.

There is are sequences
t_{p}\rightarrow+\infty
,
b_{p}\rightarrow b
and
\xi_{p}\rightarrow\xi
of elements of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\mathfrak{k}
such that
(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t_{p}\xi_{p}}\cdot b_{p})
is bounded in
V
.

4.

\left\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t\xi}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{b}\right\|_{L^{2}}
is bounded when
t\rightarrow+\infty
.

Moreover, in this case, if we set
b_{\infty}=\lim_{t\rightarrow+\infty}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t\xi}\cdot b\in B_{4,0}
, all the embeddings
(G_{\xi,\lambda},\overline{\partial}_{b_{\infty}})\subset\mathcal{E}_{b_{\infty}}
are holomorphic and for all
t
,
\left\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t\xi}\cdot b\right\|\leqslant\left\|b\right\|
. In particular,
b_{\infty}\in B
.

Proof.

1\Rightarrow 2
Assume the first point. Let
\mathrm{Sp}(\xi)=\{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}\}
with
\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}<\ldots<\lambda_{m}
. It means that in the orthogonal decomposition

E=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}G_{\xi,\lambda_{k}},

we have

\Phi(b)=\overline{\partial}_{b}-\overline{\partial}_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma_{11}&\gamma_{12}&\ldots&\gamma_{1m}\\
0&\gamma_{22}&\ldots&\gamma_{2m}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\ldots&\gamma_{mm}\end{pmatrix}

because each
F_{\xi,\lambda_{k}}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}G_{\xi,\lambda_{i}}\subset E
is preserved by both
\overline{\partial}_{0}
and
\overline{\partial}_{b}
. Let
\mathrm{Id}_{k}
be the identity of
G_{\xi,\lambda_{k}}
. We have, for all
t
,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t\xi}\cdot\Phi(b)

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t\xi}\Phi(b)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-t\xi}

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\lambda_{1}t}\mathrm{Id}_{1}&0&\ldots&0\\
0&\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\lambda_{2}t}\mathrm{Id}_{2}&\ldots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\ldots&\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\lambda_{m}t}\mathrm{Id}_{m}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\gamma_{11}&\gamma_{12}&\ldots&\gamma_{1m}\\
0&\gamma_{22}&\ldots&\gamma_{2m}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\ldots&\gamma_{mm}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-\lambda_{1}t}\mathrm{Id}_{1}&0&\ldots&0\\
0&\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-\lambda_{2}t}\mathrm{Id}_{2}&\ldots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\ldots&\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-\lambda_{m}t}\mathrm{Id}_{m}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\gamma_{11}&\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1})t}\gamma_{12}&\ldots&\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-(\lambda_{m}-\lambda_{1})t}\gamma_{1m}\\
0&\gamma_{22}&\ldots&\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-(\lambda_{m}-\lambda_{2})t}\gamma_{2m}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\ldots&\gamma_{mm}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\underset{t\rightarrow+\infty}{\longrightarrow}\

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\gamma_{11}&0&\ldots&0\\
0&\gamma_{22}&\ldots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\ldots&\gamma_{mm}\end{pmatrix}.

The decomposition
E=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}G_{\xi,\lambda_{k}}
is orthogonal and
\overline{\partial}_{0}
-holomorphic thus the associated decomposition

\Omega^{0,1}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))=\bigoplus_{i,j=1}^{m}\Omega^{0,1}(X,\mathrm{Hom}(G_{\xi,\lambda_{j}},G_{\xi,\lambda_{i}}))

is orthogonal for the Hermitian product
\left<\cdot,\cdot\right>_{L^{2}}
. We deduce that
t\mapsto\left\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t\xi}\cdot\Phi(b)\right\|_{L^{2}}
is non-increasing. Let
B^{\prime}
be an open ball in
V
such that
B\subsetneq B^{\prime}\subsetneq B_{4,0}
. As
\Phi
is an embedding and
\partial B^{\prime}
is compact and does not contain
0
,

a=\inf_{\partial B^{\prime}}\left\|\Phi(b)\right\|_{L^{2}}

is positive. Up to shrinking
B
, we may assume that for all
b\in B
,
\left\|\Phi(b)\right\|_{L^{2}}<a
. In particular, if
b\in B
, for all
t\geqslant 0
,
\left\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t\xi}\cdot\Phi(b)\right\|_{L^{2}}<a
. It implies that whenever
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t\xi}\cdot b\in B_{4,0}
, it is not in
\partial B^{\prime}
(recall that
\Phi
is
G
-invariant). Thus, it stays in the compact subset
\overline{B^{\prime}}
of
B_{4,0}
. In particular, it implies that for all non-negative time,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t\xi}\cdot b\in\overline{B^{\prime}}\subset B_{4,0}
and it converges because
\Phi(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t\xi}\cdot b)
converges and
\Phi_{|\overline{B^{\prime}}}
is injective and proper.

Moreover, the limit structure
\overline{\partial}_{b_{\infty}}
is diagonal in the decomposition
E=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}G_{\xi,\lambda_{k}}
, each
G_{\xi,\lambda_{k}}
is preserved hence the holomorphic decomposition

\mathcal{E}_{b_{\infty}}=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}(G_{\xi,\lambda_{k}},\overline{\partial}_{b_{\infty}}).

2\Rightarrow 3
and
4
Trivial.

3\Rightarrow 2
First of all, the set of all
\eta
which are diagonal in the decomposition

\mathcal{E}_{0}=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}(G_{\xi,\lambda_{k}},\overline{\partial}_{0})

is the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{t}
of a complex torus
T
in
G
. Therefore, it is contained in the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{t}_{\max}
of a maximal torus
T_{\max}
of
G
. It can be obtained thanks to an orthogonal and
\overline{\partial}_{0}
-holomorphic decomposition of each
(G_{\xi,\lambda_{k}},\overline{\partial}_{0})
for example. By assumption,
\xi\in\mathfrak{t}\subset\mathfrak{t}_{\max}
. Since
\xi_{p}\rightarrow\xi
, we can diagonalise it as

\xi_{p}=u_{p}\xi_{p}^{\prime}u_{p}^{\dagger},

where
u_{p}\in K
and
\xi_{p}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{t}_{\max}
. Moreover, as
\xi_{p}\rightarrow\xi\in\mathfrak{t}_{\max}
,
\xi_{p}^{\prime}\rightarrow\xi
and we may assume that
u_{p}\rightarrow\mathrm{Id}_{E}
. Let us write, in the orthogonal decomposition
E=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}G_{\xi,\lambda_{k}}
,

\xi_{p}^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{p,1}&0&\ldots&0\\
0&\lambda_{p,2}&\ldots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\ldots&\lambda_{p,m}\end{pmatrix}.

There is no reason to believe that
\xi_{p}^{\prime}
is in
\mathfrak{t}
so each
\lambda_{p,k}
is a holomorphic Hermitian endomorphism of
(G_{\xi,\lambda_{k}},\overline{\partial}_{0})
but it needs not be a homothety. In other words, it is block diagonal but not diagonal. As
\xi_{p}^{\prime}\rightarrow\xi
, we have
\lambda_{p,k}\rightarrow\lambda_{k}\mathrm{Id}_{k}
for all
k
. Let for all
p
,
b_{p}^{\prime}=u_{p}^{\dagger}\cdot b_{p}
and let us set

b=\begin{pmatrix}b_{11}&b_{12}&\ldots&b_{1m}\\
b_{21}&b_{22}&\ldots&b_{2m}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
b_{m1}&b_{m2}&\ldots&b_{mm}\end{pmatrix},\qquad b_{p}^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}b_{p,11}&b_{p,12}&\ldots&b_{p,1m}\\
b_{p,21}&b_{p,22}&\ldots&b_{p,2m}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
b_{p,m1}&b_{p,m2}&\ldots&b_{p,mm}\end{pmatrix}.

As
u_{p}\rightarrow\mathrm{Id}_{E}
, for all
i,j
,
b_{p,ij}\rightarrow b_{ij}
. We have, for all
p
,

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t_{p}\xi_{p}}\cdot b_{p}=u_{p}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t_{p}\xi_{p}^{\prime}}\cdot b_{p}^{\prime}=u_{p}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t_{p}\lambda_{p,1}}b_{p,11}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-t_{p}\lambda_{p,1}}&\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t_{p}\lambda_{p,1}}b_{p,12}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-t_{p}\lambda_{p,2}}&\ldots&\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t_{p}\lambda_{p,1}}b_{p,1m}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-t_{p}\lambda_{p,m}}\\
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t_{p}\lambda_{p,2}}b_{p,21}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-t_{p}\lambda_{p,1}}&\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t_{p}\lambda_{p,2}}b_{p,22}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-t_{p}\lambda_{p,2}}&\ldots&\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t_{p}\lambda_{p,2}}b_{p,2m}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-t_{p}\lambda_{p,m}}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t_{p}\lambda_{p,m}}b_{p,m1}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-t_{p}\lambda_{p,1}}&\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t_{p}\lambda_{p,m}}b_{p,m2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-t_{p}\lambda_{p,2}}&\ldots&\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t_{p}\lambda_{p,m}}b_{p,mm}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-t_{p}\lambda_{p,m}}\end{pmatrix}.

By assumption, this sequence is bounded so for all
i,j
,
(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t_{p}\lambda_{p,i}}b_{p,ij}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-t_{p}\lambda_{p,j}})
is bounded. Let
i>j
, so
\lambda_{i}>\lambda_{j}
. Set
\varepsilon_{p,i}=\lambda_{p,i}-\lambda_{i}\mathrm{Id}_{i}\rightarrow 0
and
\varepsilon_{p,j}=\lambda_{p,j}-\lambda_{j}\mathrm{Id}_{j}\rightarrow 0
. We have

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t_{p}\lambda_{p,i}}b_{p,ij}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-t_{p}\lambda_{p,j}}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t_{p}\varepsilon_{p,i}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j})t_{p}}b_{p,ij}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-t_{p}\varepsilon_{p,j}}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\geqslant\left\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-t_{p}\varepsilon_{p,i}}\right\|^{-1}\left\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t_{p}\varepsilon_{p,j}}\right\|^{-1}\left\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j})t_{p}}b_{p,ij}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\geqslant\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-t_{p}\left\|\varepsilon_{p,i}\right\|}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-t_{p}\left\|\varepsilon_{p,j}\right\|}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j})t_{p}}\left\|b_{p,ij}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\geqslant\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\frac{1}{2}(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j})t_{p}}\left\|b_{p,ij}\right\|_{L^{2}}\textrm{ for $p$ large enough.}

Since this sequence is bounded, we must have
\left\|b_{p,ij}\right\|_{L^{2}}\rightarrow 0
so
b_{ij}=0
. It holds for all
i>j
so
b
is upper diagonal. Moreover, the decomposition

\Omega^{0,1}(X,\mathrm{End}(E))=\bigoplus_{i,j=1}^{m}\Omega^{0,1}(X,\mathrm{Hom}(G_{j},G_{i}))

is orthogonal. It implies that

t\mapsto\left\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t\xi}\cdot b\right\|_{L^{2}}=\sum_{i\leqslant j}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j})}\left\|b_{ij}\right\|_{L^{2}}

decreases and
b_{\infty}=\lim_{t\rightarrow+\infty}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t\xi}\cdot b
exists and is given by the diagonal part of
b
. In particular, for all
t
,
\left\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t\xi}\cdot b\right\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\left\|b\right\|_{L^{2}}
and
\left\|b_{\infty}\right\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\left\|b\right\|_{L^{2}}
so
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t\xi}\cdot b
and
b_{\infty}
lie in
B\subset B_{4,0}
.

4\Rightarrow 1
Write, in the orthogonal decomposition
E=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}G_{\xi,\lambda_{k}}
,

\Phi(b)=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma_{11}&\gamma_{12}&\ldots&\gamma_{1m}\\
\gamma_{21}&\gamma_{22}&\ldots&\gamma_{2m}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\gamma_{m1}&\gamma_{m2}&\ldots&\gamma_{mm}\end{pmatrix}.

As before, we have, for all
t
,

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t\xi}\cdot\Phi(b)=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma_{11}&\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1})t}\gamma_{12}&\ldots&\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-(\lambda_{m}-\lambda_{1})t}\gamma_{1m}\\
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1})t}\gamma_{21}&\gamma_{22}&\ldots&\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-(\lambda_{m}-\lambda_{2})t}\gamma_{2m}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{(\lambda_{m}-\lambda_{1})t}\gamma_{m1}&\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{(\lambda_{m}-\lambda_{2})t}\gamma_{m2}&\ldots&\gamma_{mm}\end{pmatrix}.

We easily see that if
\left\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t\xi}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{b}\right\|_{L^{2}}
is bounded for large
t
, then
\gamma_{ij}=0
when
i>j
. We deduce that each
F_{\xi,\lambda_{k}}
is preserved by
\overline{\partial}_{b}=\overline{\partial}_{0}+\Phi(b)
thus
(F_{\xi,\lambda_{k}},\overline{\partial}_{b})\subset\mathcal{E}_{b}
is holomorphic.
∎

The following lemma will be useful to show uniqueness results.

Lemma 5.4
.

Up to shrinking
B
, if
b\in B
and
\xi\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\mathfrak{k}
are such that
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\xi}\cdot b\in B
, then, all
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t\xi}\cdot b
for
0\leqslant t\leqslant 1
lie in
B_{4,0}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathrm{Sp}(\xi)=\{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}\}
with
\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}<\ldots<\lambda_{m}
so

\xi=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\lambda_{k}\Pi_{\lambda_{k}}.

Similarly as in the proof of Proposition
5.3
, we have an orthogonal decomposition

E=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}G_{\xi,\lambda_{k}}

in which we can decompose
b
as

b=\begin{pmatrix}b_{11}&b_{12}&\ldots&b_{1m}\\
b_{21}&b_{22}&\ldots&b_{2m}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
b_{m1}&b_{m2}&\ldots&b_{mm}\end{pmatrix}.

So for all
t
, we have,

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t\xi}\cdot b=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t\xi}b\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-t\xi}=\begin{pmatrix}b_{11}&\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})t}b_{12}&\ldots&\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{m})t}b_{1m}\\
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1})t}b_{21}&b_{22}&\ldots&\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{m})t}b_{2m}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{(\lambda_{m}-\lambda_{1})t}b_{m1}&\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{(\lambda_{m}-\lambda_{2})t}b_{m2}&\ldots&b_{mm}\end{pmatrix}.

In particular, for
t=1
,

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\xi}\cdot b=\begin{pmatrix}b_{11}&\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}b_{12}&\ldots&\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{m}}b_{1m}\\
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1}}b_{21}&b_{22}&\ldots&\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{m}}b_{2m}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\lambda_{m}-\lambda_{1}}b_{m1}&\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\lambda_{m}-\lambda_{2}}b_{m2}&\ldots&b_{mm}\end{pmatrix}.

And for all
i,j
and
0\leqslant t\leqslant 1
,

\left\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j})t}b_{ij}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{2(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j})t}\left\|b_{ij}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leqslant\max\{\left\|b_{ij}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2},\left\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}}b_{ij}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\}\leqslant\left\|b_{ij}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}}b_{ij}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

When we sum the last inequality for all
1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m
, we obtain that

\left\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t\xi}\cdot b\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leqslant\left\|b\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\xi}\cdot b\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Therefore, if the radius of
B
is less than or equal to the radius of
B_{4,0}
divided by
\sqrt{2}
, the result of the lemma holds, which we may assume up to shrinking
B
.
∎

From now on, we set
B_{3,0}\subset B_{4,0}
which satisfies Proposition
5.3
and Lemma
5.4
. Once again, it is fixed and we still call
B
a smaller ball that we allow ourselves to shrink later.

5.2
Construction of
\mathcal{B}_{d}

In this sub-section, we show that that all zeroes of
\mu_{\infty,\zeta}
which are
\mathcal{C}^{d}
close to
\overline{\partial}_{0}
and in the gauge orbit of
\overline{\partial}_{b}
for some
b\in B
actually belong to the deformed slice
\tilde{\Phi}(\zeta,B_{3,0})
up to a unitary gauge transform. We define here the open neighbourhood
\mathcal{B}_{d}
of
\overline{\partial}_{0}
mentioned in Theorem
4.12
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
B^{\prime\prime}\subset B_{3,0}
be an open ball centred at
0
. There is a ball
B^{\prime}\subset B^{\prime\prime}
such that for all
\alpha\in\mathrm{im}(\overline{\partial}_{0}^{\bullet})
whose
\mathcal{C}^{0}
norm is small enough, for all
f\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}(E))
and all
(b,b^{\prime})\in{B^{\prime}}^{2}
, if

f\cdot\overline{\partial}_{b}=\overline{\partial}_{b^{\prime}}+\alpha,

then
f\in G
and
f\cdot b\in B^{\prime\prime}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.3
,
\Phi
takes values in
\ker(\overline{\partial}_{0}^{\bullet})
and since
\overline{\partial}_{0}
is integrable,
\mathrm{im}(\overline{\partial}_{0}^{\bullet})\subset\ker(\overline{\partial}_{0}^{\bullet})
. When
b\in B_{3,0}
and
\alpha\in\mathrm{im}(\overline{\partial}_{0}^{\bullet})
, the complex structure of
\mathrm{Hom}((E,\overline{\partial}_{b}),(E,\overline{\partial}_{b^{\prime}}+\alpha))
is given by

\overline{\partial}_{b,b^{\prime},\alpha}f=(\overline{\partial}_{b^{\prime}}+\alpha)\circ f-f\circ\overline{\partial}_{b}=\overline{\partial}_{0}f+(\Phi(b^{\prime})+\alpha)f-f\Phi(b).

Since
\Phi(b)
,
\Phi(b^{\prime})
and
\alpha
are in
\ker(\overline{\partial}_{0}^{\bullet})
, then
\overline{\partial}_{b,b^{\prime},\alpha}-\overline{\partial}_{0}\in\ker(\overline{\partial}_{0}^{\bullet})
(be careful, this time, we are in
\mathrm{End}(E)
, not in
E
). In particular, we may apply Lemma
5.1
to
\mathcal{F}=\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{E}_{0})
so, when
b
,
b^{\prime}
and the
\mathcal{C}^{0}
norm of
\alpha
are small enough, any
f\in\ker(\overline{\partial}_{b,b^{\prime},\alpha})
is in
\ker(\overline{\partial}_{0})
.

Let
B^{\prime}\subset B_{3,0}
and
\varepsilon>0
such that this property is true when
(b,b^{\prime})\in{B^{\prime}}^{2}
and
\left\|\alpha\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}\leqslant\varepsilon
. Assume without loss of generality that
B^{\prime}\subset B^{\prime\prime}
.

By Proposition
4.3
,
\Phi(b)=b+\mathrm{O}(\left\|b\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2})
thus there is a constant
C>0
such that, up to shrinking
B^{\prime}
, for all
b\in B^{\prime}
,

\left\|\Phi(b)-b\right\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant C\left\|b\right\|_{L^{2}}.

(6)

Moreover,
\Phi(b)-b\in\mathrm{im}(\overline{\partial}_{0}^{\bullet})\subset V^{\bot}
and the action of
G
by conjugation preserves both
V
and
V^{\bot}
thus, for all
b\in B^{\prime}
and all
f\in G
,

\left\|fbf^{-1}\right\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\left\|f\Phi(b)f^{-1}\right\|_{L^{2}}.

(7)

Let
C^{\prime}>0
be a constant such that, on
(0,1)
-forms,

\left\|\cdot\right\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant C^{\prime}\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}.

(8)

Let
r^{\prime}\leqslant r^{\prime\prime}
the respective radii of
B^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime\prime}
. Assume up to reducing
\varepsilon
and shrinking
B^{\prime}
that

r^{\prime}+C{r^{\prime}}^{2}+C^{\prime}\varepsilon\leqslant r^{\prime\prime}.

(9)

We can now prove the lemma. Let
(b,b^{\prime})\in{B^{\prime}}^{2}
,
\alpha\in\mathrm{im}(\overline{\partial}_{0}^{\bullet})
with
\left\|\alpha\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}\leqslant\varepsilon
and
f\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}(E)
such that
f\cdot\overline{\partial}_{b}=\overline{\partial}_{b^{\prime}}+\alpha
. We have
f\in\ker(\overline{\partial}_{b,b^{\prime},\alpha})
so by construction of
B^{\prime}
and
\varepsilon
,
f\in G
and we have

f\Phi(b)f^{-1}=\Phi(b^{\prime})+\alpha.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\left\|fbf^{-1}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\|f\Phi(b)f^{-1}\right\|_{L^{2}}\textrm{ by (\ref{EQ:Inégalité norme fPhi(b)f-1}),}

\displaystyle=\left\|\Phi(b^{\prime})+\alpha\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\|b^{\prime}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\Phi(b^{\prime})-b^{\prime}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\alpha\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\|b^{\prime}\right\|_{L^{2}}+C\left\|b^{\prime}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+C^{\prime}\left\|\alpha\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}\textrm{ by (\ref{EQ:Inégalité norme Phi(b) - b}) and (\ref{EQ:Norme L2 dominée}),}

\displaystyle<r^{\prime\prime}\textrm{ by (\ref{EQ:Inégalité rayons}).}

It proves that
f\cdot b=fbf^{-1}\in B^{\prime\prime}
.
∎

Proposition 5.6
.

Up to shrinking
B
and
\mathcal{U}_{d}
, there is an open neighbourhood
\mathcal{B}_{d}
of
\overline{\partial}_{0}
in
\mathcal{X}_{d}
such that
\overline{\partial}_{0}+\tilde{\Phi}(\mathcal{U}_{d},B)\subset\mathcal{B}_{d}
. Reciprocally, there is a smooth map

\beta:\mathcal{B}_{d}\rightarrow B_{3,0},

such that for all
b\in B
and all
\overline{\partial}\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}(E)\cdot\overline{\partial}_{b}\cap\mathcal{B}_{d}
,
\beta(\overline{\partial})\in G\cdot b
. If moreover,
\mu_{\infty,\zeta}(\overline{\partial})=0
, there is a unitary gauge transform
u\in\mathcal{G}(E,h)
such that
\overline{\partial}=u\cdot(\overline{\partial}_{0}+\tilde{\Phi}(\zeta,\beta(\overline{\partial})))
and
\tilde{\mu}_{\zeta}(\beta(\overline{\partial}))=0
.

Proof.

Consider the germ of smooth map

\Psi:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle(B_{3,0}\times\mathcal{C}^{d+1}(\mathfrak{g}^{\bot})\times\mathcal{C}^{d}(\mathrm{im}(\overline{\partial}_{0}^{\bullet})),(0,0,0))&\longrightarrow&\displaystyle(\mathcal{X}_{d},\overline{\partial}_{0})\\
\displaystyle(b,s,\alpha)&\longmapsto&\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\Im(s)}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\Re(s)}\cdot(\overline{\partial}_{b}+\alpha)\end{array}\right..

We have

d\Psi(0,0,0)(v,s,\alpha)=v-\overline{\partial}_{0}s+\alpha,

which is an isomorphism by Hodge decomposition (
5
). Therefore, by the inverse function theorem, there are a neighbourhood
B^{\prime\prime}
of
0
in
B_{3,0}
, a neighbourhood
\mathcal{M}
of
0
in
\mathcal{C}^{d+1}(\mathfrak{g}^{\bot})
, a neighbourhood
\mathcal{N}
of
0
in
\mathcal{C}^{d}(\mathrm{im}(\overline{\partial}_{0}^{\bullet}))
and a neighbourhood
\mathcal{B}_{d}^{\prime}
of
\overline{\partial}_{0}
in
\mathcal{X}_{d}
such that

\Psi:B^{\prime\prime}\times\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{N}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{d}^{\prime}

is a smooth diffeomorphism. Let
B^{\prime}\subset B^{\prime\prime}
given by Lemma
5.5
. Since the
\mathcal{C}^{0}
norm dominates the
\mathcal{C}^{d}
norm, we may assume up to shrinking
\mathcal{N}
(thus
\mathcal{B}_{d}^{\prime}
) that all
\alpha\in\mathcal{N}
satisfy Lemma
5.5
with
B^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime\prime}
. We may also assume up to shrining
\mathcal{M}
(thus
\mathcal{B}_{d}^{\prime}
) that the Hermitian part of all
s\in\mathcal{M}
verify the third point of Proposition
4.5
. Let

\mathcal{B}_{d}=\Psi(B^{\prime}\times\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{N}).

Up to shrinking
B
and
\mathcal{U}_{d}
, we may assume that
B\subset B^{\prime}
and
\overline{\partial}_{0}+\tilde{\Phi}(\mathcal{U}_{d},B)\subset\mathcal{B}_{d}
so the first condition is verified. We then define
\beta
as the smooth map given by the first component of the local inverse of
\Psi
.

\beta=(\Psi^{-1})_{1}:\mathcal{B}_{d}\rightarrow B^{\prime}\subset B_{3,0}.

Let
\zeta\in\mathcal{U}_{d}
,
b\in B\subset B^{\prime}
and
g\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}(E)
such that
\overline{\partial}=g\cdot\overline{\partial}_{b}\in\mathcal{B}_{d}
. Let
(b^{\prime},s,\alpha)=\Psi^{-1}(\overline{\partial})\in B^{\prime}\times\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{N}
so
b^{\prime}=\beta(\overline{\partial})
and
g\cdot\overline{\partial}_{b}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\Im(s)}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\Re(s)}\cdot(\overline{\partial}_{b^{\prime}}+\alpha)
. Let
f=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-\Re(s)}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\Im(s)}g\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}(E)
so

f\cdot\overline{\partial}_{b}=\overline{\partial}_{b^{\prime}}+\alpha.

By Lemma
5.5
,
f\in G
and
f\cdot b\in B^{\prime\prime}
hence
f\cdot\overline{\partial}_{b}=\overline{\partial}_{f\cdot b}
and since
\Psi
is injective on
B^{\prime\prime}\times\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{N}
, the equality
\overline{\partial}_{f\cdot b}=\overline{\partial}_{b^{\prime}}+\alpha
implies
f\cdot b=b^{\prime}
and
\alpha=0
. In particular,
b^{\prime}\in G\cdot b
as wanted.

Assume now that
\mu_{\infty,\zeta}(\overline{\partial})=0
. Recall that
\overline{\partial}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\Im(s)}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\Re(s)}\cdot(\overline{\partial}_{b^{\prime}}+\alpha)=u\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\Re(s)}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{b^{\prime}}
with
u=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\Im(s)}\in\mathcal{C}^{d+1}(\mathcal{G}(E,h))
. As
\overline{\partial}
is
P_{\zeta}
-critical, so is
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\Re(s)}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{b^{\prime}}
. We have
s\in\mathcal{M}
and
\mu_{\infty,\zeta}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\Re(s)}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{b})=0
so by Proposition
4.5
,
\Re(s)=\sigma(\zeta,b^{\prime})
so
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\Re(s)}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{b^{\prime}}=\overline{\partial}_{0}+\tilde{\Phi}(\zeta,b^{\prime})
. Finally, we have

\overline{\partial}=u\cdot(\overline{\partial}_{0}+\tilde{\Phi}(\zeta,b^{\prime})),\qquad b^{\prime}=f\cdot b\in G\cdot b\cap B_{3,0}.

Moreover,
\tilde{\mu}_{\zeta}(b^{\prime})=\mu_{\infty,\zeta}(\overline{\partial}_{0}+\tilde{\Phi}(\zeta,b^{\prime}))=0
.
∎

5.3
Gradient flows of the moment map squared, existence and convergence

In this sub-section, we define and study the negative gradient flows of the moment maps. In particular, we show that the flows associated with
\mu_{\zeta_{0}}
and
\tilde{\mu}_{\zeta_{0}}
are well-defined at all time if the starting point is close enough to
0
. It relies on Łojasiewicz gradient inequality and Duistermaat’s theorem. In the Kähler case (
i.e.
for
\mu_{\zeta_{0}}
), this result is due to Ortu
[
38
, Proposition 2.4]
. In
[
38
]
, Ortu studies cscK like equations but her results
[
38
, Sub-section 2.1]
apply for general moment map frameworks. In the non-Kähler case (
i.e.
for
\tilde{\mu}_{\zeta_{0}}
), the proof is similar but we include it for completeness.

Let
\zeta\in\mathcal{U}_{d}
and
\phi_{\zeta}
(resp.
\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}
) be the flows defined by

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\phi_{\zeta}(b,0)&=&b,\\
\frac{\partial\phi_{\zeta}}{\partial t}(b,t)&=&-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}L_{\phi_{\zeta}(b,t)}\mu_{\zeta}(\phi_{\zeta}(b,t)).\end{array}\right.\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,0)&=&b,\\
\frac{\partial\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}}{\partial t}(b,t)&=&-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}L_{\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t)}\tilde{\mu}_{\zeta}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t)).\end{array}\right..

Recall that when
b\in B
and
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
,
L_{b}\xi
is the infinitesimal action of
\xi
at
b
given by

L_{b}\xi=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}|_{t=0}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t\xi}\cdot b)=[\xi,b]\in T_{b}B.

Let for all
\zeta
,

f_{\zeta}:b\mapsto\frac{1}{2}\left\|\mu_{\zeta}(b)\right\|^{2},\qquad\tilde{f}_{\zeta}:b\mapsto\frac{1}{2}\left\|\tilde{\mu}_{\zeta}(b)\right\|^{2},

be (half) the squares of the norms of the moment maps. The following lemmas are
[
24
, Lemma 3.1, Lemma 3.2]
, the proofs are similar.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
b\in B
,
v\in T_{b}B
and
\zeta\in\mathcal{U}_{d}
. The flows
\phi_{\zeta}
and
\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}
verify, where they are defined,

\Omega_{\zeta,\phi_{\zeta}(b,t)}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\partial\phi_{\zeta}}{\partial t}(b,t),v\right)=df_{\zeta}(\phi_{\zeta}(b,t))v,\qquad\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta,\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t)}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\partial\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}}{\partial t}(b,t),v\right)=d\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t))v.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
b\in B
and
\zeta\in\mathcal{U}_{d}
. Let
g
(resp.
\tilde{g}
) in
G
be defined by

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}g(0)&=&\mathrm{Id}_{E},\\
g(t)^{-1}g^{\prime}(t)&=&\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\mu_{\zeta}(\phi_{\zeta}(b,t)).\end{array}\right.\qquad\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\tilde{g}(0)&=&\mathrm{Id}_{E},\\
\tilde{g}(t)^{-1}\tilde{g}^{\prime}(t)&=&\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\tilde{\mu}_{\zeta}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t)).\end{array}\right..

They verify, where they are defined,

\phi_{\zeta}(b,t)=g(t)^{-1}\cdot b,\qquad\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t)=\tilde{g}(t)^{-1}\cdot b.

In particular,
G
-orbits are preserved by
\phi_{\zeta}
and
\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}
.

The first equality in Lemma
5.7
can be rewritten as

\frac{\partial\phi_{\zeta}}{\partial t}(b,t)=-\nabla_{\Omega_{\zeta}}f_{\zeta}(\phi_{\zeta}(b,t)),

where
\nabla_{\Omega}
is the gradient with respect to the scalar product induced by
\Omega_{\zeta}
. Similarly, a consequence of Lemma
4.7
is that
\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta}
is a symplectic form. In particular, we can define

-\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta,b}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\nabla_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta}}\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(b),v\right)=d\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(b)v.

In this case, the second equality in Lemma
5.7
can be rewritten as

\frac{\partial\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}}{\partial t}(b,t)=-\nabla_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta}}\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t)).

(10)

Notice that
\nabla_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta}}\tilde{f}_{\zeta}
shares properties with the usual gradients of
\tilde{f}
. Clearly, it vanishes at some point
b
if and only if
b
is a critical point of
\tilde{f}_{\zeta}
. Moreover, by Lemma
4.7
, there is a positive constant
c
such that

d\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(b)\nabla_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta}}\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(b)=-\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta,b}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\nabla_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta}}\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(b),\nabla_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta}}\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(b))\geqslant c\left\|\nabla_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta}}\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(b)\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\geqslant 0

(11)

with equality if and only if
\nabla_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta}}\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(b)=0
. We easily see that it implies that
t\mapsto\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t))
is non-increasing (similarly with
t\mapsto f_{\zeta}(\phi_{\zeta}(b,t))
). Using this, if
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{L^{2}(V^{\lor})}
is the operator norm on the dual space
V^{\lor}
of
V
induced by
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{L^{2}}
, we have

\left\|d\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(b)\right\|_{L^{2}(V^{\lor})}\geqslant\frac{\left|d\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(b)\nabla_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta}}\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(b)\right|}{\left\|\nabla_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta}}\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(b)\right\|_{L^{2}}}\geqslant c\left\|\nabla_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta}}\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(b)\right\|_{L^{2}}.

(12)

However,
\nabla_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta}}\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(b)
is
a priori
not a gradient of
\tilde{f}_{\zeta}
because the non-degenerate positive forms
(v,w)\mapsto\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta,b}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}v,w)
need not be symmetric as
\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta}
is not compatible with the complex structure on
B
.

We now study the existence of these flows at
\zeta=\zeta_{0}
. A key tool is the Łojasiewicz gradient inequality.

Proposition 5.9

(Łojasiewicz gradient inequality,
[
49
, Chapter 18, Proposition 1]
)
.

If
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is open and
g:U\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is real analytic, then for all
x\in U
critical point of
g
, there is a neighbourhood
V
of
x
in
U
and constants
a>0
and
\frac{1}{2}\leqslant\theta<1
such that, for all
y\in V
,

a\left|g(x)-g(y)\right|^{\theta}\leqslant\left\|\nabla g(y)\right\|.

Using the Marle–Guillemin–Sternberg form
[
31
, Theorem 2.1]
, we can show that the norms squared of the moment maps are locally real analytic up to a composition on the right with a smooth local diffeomorphism. It means that we can apply Proposition
5.9
to the functions
f_{\zeta_{0}}
and
\tilde{f}_{\zeta_{0}}
at
b=0
. Indeed,
\mu_{\zeta_{0}}(0)=\tilde{\mu}_{\zeta_{0}}(0)=0
thus
f_{\zeta_{0}}
and
\tilde{f}_{\zeta_{0}}
have a critical point at
0
. Moreover, by (
12
), we can replace the gradient of
\tilde{f}_{\zeta_{0}}
by
\nabla_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta_{0}}}\tilde{f}_{\zeta_{0}}
. Thus, up to shrinking
B
, there are constants
a>0
and
\frac{1}{2}\leqslant\theta<1
such that for all
b\in B
,

af_{\zeta_{0}}(b)^{\theta}\leqslant\left\|\nabla_{\Omega_{\zeta_{0}}}f_{\zeta_{0}}(b)\right\|_{L^{2}},\qquad a\tilde{f}_{\zeta_{0}}(b)^{\theta}\leqslant\left\|\nabla_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta_{0}}}\tilde{f}_{\zeta_{0}}(b)\right\|_{L^{2}}.

(13)

In particular, all critical points of
f_{\zeta_{0}}
or
\tilde{f}_{\zeta_{0}}
in
B
are zeroes of the associated moment maps.

Łojasiewicz gradient inequality is the last assumption we needed on the elements of
b
. Let
B_{2,0}=B
which satisfies Propositions
5.6
and
5.9
. Let
B_{2}\subset B_{2,0}
. We will now construct
B_{1}\subset B_{2}
in function of
B_{2}
which satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
4.12
. In particular, we want the flows
\phi_{\zeta_{0}}
and
\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta_{0}}
starting at points of
B_{1}
to stay in
B_{2}
. For now, set
B_{1}=B_{2}
, but we allow ourselves to shrink it.

Proposition 5.10

(
[
38
, Proposition 2.4]
)
.

Up to shrinking
B_{1}
,
\phi_{\zeta_{0}}(b,t)
(resp.
\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta_{0}}(b,t)
) are well-defined in
B_{2}
for all non-negative time as long as
b\in B_{1}
. Moreover, they stay in some compact subset of
B_{2}
(independent from
b\in B_{1}
).

Proof.

The proof is the same for
\phi_{\zeta_{0}}
and
\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta_{0}}
so we prove it for
\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta_{0}}
to emphasize the fact that the non-holomorphy of
\tilde{\Phi}(\zeta_{0},\cdot)
is not a problem here. The proof is similar to the proof of
[
24
, Theorem 3.3]
. Let
b\in B_{1}
. If
\tilde{f}_{\zeta_{0}}(b)=0
, then
\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta_{0}}(b,\cdot)=b
is constant and defined for all
t\geqslant 0
. Else, for all
t
where
\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta_{0}}(b,t)
is defined,
\tilde{f}_{\zeta_{0}}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta_{0}}(b,t))\neq 0
because zeroes of
\tilde{f}_{\zeta_{0}}
are critical points and

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}(\tilde{f}_{\zeta_{0}}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta_{0}}(b,t))^{1-\theta})

\displaystyle=-(1-\theta)\tilde{f}_{\zeta_{0}}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta_{0}}(b,t))^{-\theta}d\tilde{f}_{\zeta_{0}}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta_{0}}(b,t))\frac{\partial\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta_{0}}}{\partial t}(b,t)

\displaystyle=(1-\theta)\tilde{f}_{\zeta_{0}}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta_{0}}(b,t))^{-\theta}d\tilde{f}_{\zeta_{0}}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta_{0}}(b,t))\nabla_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta_{0}}}\tilde{f}_{\zeta_{0}}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta_{0}}(b,t))\textrm{ by (\ref{EQ:Flux de gradient}),}

\displaystyle\geqslant c(1-\theta)\tilde{f}_{\zeta_{0}}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta_{0}}(b,t))^{-\theta}\left\|\nabla_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta_{0}}}\tilde{f}_{\zeta_{0}}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta_{0}}(b,t))\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\textrm{ by (\ref{EQ:Norme gradient tilde}),}

\displaystyle\geqslant ac(1-\theta)\left\|\nabla_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta_{0}}}\tilde{f}_{\zeta_{0}}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta_{0}}(b,t))\right\|_{L^{2}}\textrm{ by (\ref{EQ:Lojasiewicz}),}

\displaystyle=ac(1-\theta)\left\|\frac{\partial\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta_{0}}}{\partial t}(b,t)\right\|_{L^{2}}.

When we integrate this on an interval
[t_{1},t_{2}]
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta_{0}}(b,t_{1})-\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta_{0}}(b,t_{2})\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\left\|\frac{\partial\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta_{0}}}{\partial s}(b,s)\right\|_{L^{2}}\,ds

\displaystyle\leqslant-\frac{1}{ac(1-\theta)}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial s}(\tilde{f}_{\zeta_{0}}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta_{0}}(b,s))^{1-\theta})\,ds

\displaystyle=\frac{-\tilde{f}_{\zeta_{0}}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta_{0}}(b,t_{2}))^{1-\theta}+\tilde{f}_{\zeta_{0}}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta_{0}}(b,t_{1}))^{1-\theta}}{ac(1-\theta)}.

(14)

In particular,

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta_{0}}(b,t)\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\|\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta_{0}}(b,t)-\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta_{0}}(b,0)\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|b\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\tilde{f}_{\zeta_{0}}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta_{0}}(b,0))^{1-\theta}-\tilde{f}_{\zeta_{0}}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta_{0}}(b,t))^{1-\theta}}{ac(1-\theta)}+\left\|b\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\tilde{f}_{\zeta_{0}}(b)^{1-\theta}}{ac(1-\theta)}+\left\|b\right\|_{L^{2}}.

(15)

Let
r>0
be the radius of
B_{2}
and chose
B_{1}
small enough so

\sup_{b\in B_{1}}\frac{\tilde{f}_{\zeta_{0}}(b)^{1-\theta}}{ac(1-\theta)}+\left\|b\right\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\frac{r}{2}.

In this case, if
b\in B_{1}
, by (
15
),
\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta_{0}}(b,t)
stays in the closed ball of centre
0
and radius
\frac{r}{2}
, which is compact. By the finite time explosion theorem, this flow is defined for all
t\geqslant 0
in
B_{2}
.
∎

We end this sub-section with a classical convergence result (see
[
24
, Theorem 3.3]
) and a continuity result which is a weak version of the Duistermaat’s theorem (see
[
31
, Theorem 1.1]
). They both rely on the Łojasiewicz gradient inequality.

Proposition 5.11
.

Let
b\in B_{2}
and
\zeta\in\mathcal{U}_{d}
. Assume that the flow
\phi_{\zeta}(b,\cdot)
(resp.
\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,\cdot)
) exists for all
t
and stays in a compact subset of
B_{2}
. Then, it converges toward a critical point of
f_{\zeta}
(resp.
\tilde{f}_{\zeta}
). Moreover, there are constants
C>0
and
\epsilon>0
(depending on
b
and
\zeta
) such that for all
t>0
,

\int_{t}^{+\infty}\left\|\frac{\partial\phi_{\zeta}(b,s)}{\partial s}\right\|_{L^{2}}\,ds\leqslant\frac{C}{t^{\epsilon}}\qquad\left(\textrm{resp. }\int_{t}^{+\infty}\left\|\frac{\partial\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,s)}{\partial s}\right\|_{L^{2}}\,ds\leqslant\frac{C}{t^{\epsilon}}\right).

Proof.

Once again, we only prove it for
\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}
because the proof is the same for
\phi_{\zeta}
. Let
K\subset B_{2}
a compact set where the flow stays at all time. Set
l=\lim_{t\rightarrow+\infty}\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t))
which exists because this function is non-negative and non-increasing. Let
b_{0}
be a critical point of
\tilde{f}_{\zeta}
such that
\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(b_{0})=l
. We use Proposition
5.9
near
b_{0}
so there is an open neighbourhood
U_{0}
of
b_{0}
in
B_{2}
such that, for all
b_{1}\in U_{0}
,

a(b_{0})\left|\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(b_{1})-l\right|^{\theta(b_{0})}\leqslant\left\|\nabla_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta}}\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(b_{1})\right\|_{L^{2}},

where
\frac{1}{2}\leqslant\theta(b_{0})<1
and
a(b_{0})>0
. Of course, such an inequality is also true near any point
b_{0}
where
\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(b_{0})=l
. The level set
K_{l}=\{b_{0}\in K|\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(b_{0})=l\}
is closed in
K
thus compact. By compactness, we can find a neighbourhood
U
of
K_{l}
in
K
and uniform constants
\frac{1}{2}\leqslant\theta^{\prime}<1
and
a^{\prime}>0
such that, for all
b_{1}\in U
,

a^{\prime}\left|\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(b_{1})-l\right|^{\theta^{\prime}}\leqslant\left\|\nabla_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta}}\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(b_{1})\right\|_{L^{2}}.

(16)

By properness of
\tilde{f}_{\zeta}
on
K
, we may choose
U
of the form

U_{\delta}=\left\{b\in K|\left|\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(b)-l\right|<\delta\right\},

for some
\delta>0
. In particular,
\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t))\underset{t\rightarrow+\infty}{\longrightarrow}l
implies that there is a
T\geqslant 0
such that for all
t\geqslant T
,
\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t)\in U_{\delta}
. Similarly to the proof of (
5.3
), we show that, for all
t_{2}\geqslant t_{1}\geqslant T
,

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t_{1})-\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t_{2})\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\left\|\frac{\partial\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,s)}{\partial s}\right\|_{L^{2}}\,ds

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{-(\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t_{2}))-l)^{1-\theta^{\prime}}+(\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t_{1}))-l)^{1-\theta^{\prime}}}{a^{\prime}c(1-\theta^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{(\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t_{1}))-l)^{1-\theta^{\prime}}}{a^{\prime}c(1-\theta^{\prime})}.

(17)

As
\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t)
stays in
K
which is complete, we can use Cauchy sequences like arguments to say that
\lim_{t\rightarrow+\infty}\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t)
exists. This point must be a critical point of
\tilde{f}_{\zeta}
.

For the last inequality, assume without loss of generality that
\theta^{\prime}>\frac{1}{2}
. We have, for all
t\geqslant T
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}((\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t))-l)^{1-2\theta^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=(1-2\theta^{\prime})(\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t))-l)^{-2\theta^{\prime}}d\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t))\frac{\partial\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}}{\partial t}(b,t)

\displaystyle=(2\theta^{\prime}-1)(\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t))-l)^{-2\theta^{\prime}}d\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t))\nabla_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta}}\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t))\textrm{ by (\ref{EQ:Flux de gradient}),}

\displaystyle\geqslant c(2\theta^{\prime}-1)(\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t))-l)^{-2\theta^{\prime}}\left\|\nabla_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta}}\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t))\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\textrm{ by (\ref{EQ:Norme gradient tilde}),}

\displaystyle\geqslant{a^{\prime}}^{2}c(2\theta^{\prime}-1)\textrm{ by (\ref{EQ:Lojasiewicz 3}).}

Therefore,
\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t)-l)^{1-2\theta^{\prime}}\geqslant{a^{\prime}}^{2}c(2\theta^{\prime}-1)(t-T)
. Finally, for all
t\geqslant T
, by (
17
),

\int_{t}^{+\infty}\left\|\frac{\partial\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,s)}{\partial s}\right\|_{L^{2}}\,ds\leqslant\frac{(\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t))-l)^{1-\theta^{\prime}}}{a^{\prime}c(1-\theta^{\prime})}\leqslant\frac{({a^{\prime}}^{2}c(2\theta^{\prime}-1)(t-T))^{\frac{1-\theta^{\prime}}{1-2\theta^{\prime}}}}{a^{\prime}c(1-\theta^{\prime})}.

We deduce the wanted inequality with
\epsilon=\frac{1-\theta^{\prime}}{2\theta^{\prime}-1}>0
when
t\geqslant 2T
for some
C>0
. Up to adjusting the constant
C
, we may assume it is also true on the compact interval
[0,2T]
.
∎

Proposition 5.12
.

Let
(\zeta,b_{0})
such that for all
b
close enough to
b_{0}
the flow
\phi_{\zeta}(b,t)
(resp.
\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t)
) exists at all time and stays in a compact subset of
B_{2}
independent of
b
. Assume moreover that
\lim_{t\mapsto+\infty}\phi_{\zeta}(b_{0},t)
is a zero of
\mu_{\zeta}
(resp.
\lim_{t\mapsto+\infty}\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b_{0},t)
is a zero of
\tilde{\mu}_{\zeta}
). Then,

b\mapsto\lim_{t\rightarrow+\infty}\phi_{\zeta}(b,t)\qquad\left(\textrm{resp. }b\mapsto\lim_{t\rightarrow+\infty}\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t)\right)

is continuous at
b_{0}
.

Proof.

As for Proposition
5.11
, we only prove it for
\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}
. Let
K\subset B_{2}
be a compact set in which the flows
\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t)
stays at all time (when
b
is close enough to
b_{0}
). As in the proof of Proposition
5.11
, there is a
\delta>0
such that for all
b
in

U_{\delta}=\left\{b\in K|\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(b)<\delta\right\},

we have the Łojasiewicz gradient inequality,

a^{\prime}f_{\zeta}(b)^{1-\theta^{\prime}}\leqslant\left\|\nabla_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\zeta}}\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(b)\right\|_{L^{2}}

for some
a^{\prime}>0
and
\frac{1}{2}\leqslant\theta^{\prime}<1
. Let
\varepsilon>0
that we assume to be smaller than
\delta
. Let
T>0
such that
\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b_{0},T))\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
. By continuity of
b\mapsto\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,T)
, for all
b
close enough to
b_{0}
(in function of
\varepsilon
),
\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,T))\leqslant\varepsilon
. Then, since
\tilde{f}_{\zeta}
non-increases with the flow, for all
b
close enough to
b_{0}
and for all
t\geqslant T
,

\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t))<\varepsilon.

In particular,
\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t)\in U_{\delta}
. As in the proof of Proposition
5.11
, we have, for all
b
close enough to
b_{0}
and for all
t\geqslant T
,

\left\|\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,T)-\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t)\right\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\frac{\tilde{f}_{\zeta}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,T))^{1-\theta^{\prime}}}{a^{\prime}c(1-\theta^{\prime})}\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon^{1-\theta^{\prime}}}{a^{\prime}c(1-\theta^{\prime})}.

In particular,

\displaystyle\left\|\lim_{t\rightarrow+\infty}\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t)-\lim_{t\rightarrow+\infty}\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b_{0},t)\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\

\displaystyle\left\|\lim_{t\rightarrow+\infty}\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t)-\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,T)\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,T)-\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b_{0},T)\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b_{0},T)-\lim_{t\rightarrow+\infty}\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b_{0},t)\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon^{1-\theta^{\prime}}}{a^{\prime}c(1-\theta^{\prime})}+\varepsilon+\frac{\varepsilon^{1-\theta^{\prime}}}{a^{\prime}c(1-\theta^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}\

\displaystyle 0.

We deduce the wanted continuity result.
∎

5.4
Closure of an orbit and equivariant arc

The following proposition gives sufficient conditions for a point in the closure of some
G
-orbit in
B
to be reachable by a
1
-parameter sub-group and will be useful to construct locally
P
-destabilising sub-bundles thanks to Proposition
5.3
. The proof shares similarities with the one of
[
24
, Theorem 12.5]
.

Proposition 5.13
.

Let
(b,b_{\infty})\in B_{2}^{2}
and
g:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow G
a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
path in
G
such that
g(t)\cdot b\underset{t\rightarrow+\infty}{\longrightarrow}b_{\infty}
. Assume that
\mathrm{Stab}(b_{\infty})=(\mathrm{Stab}(b_{\infty})\cap K)^{\mathbb{C}}
. Then, there is a
\xi\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\mathfrak{k}
and a point
b^{\prime}\in G\cdot b\cap B_{2}
such that

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t\xi}\cdot b^{\prime}\underset{t\rightarrow+\infty}{\longrightarrow}b_{\infty}.

Proof.

Let us define

G_{b_{\infty}}=\mathrm{Stab}(b_{\infty}),\qquad K_{b_{\infty}}=G_{b_{\infty}}\cap K.

Their Lie algebras are

\mathfrak{g}_{b_{\infty}}=\mathrm{Lie}(G_{b_{\infty}})=\ker(L_{b_{\infty}}),\qquad\mathfrak{k}_{b_{\infty}}=\mathrm{Lie}(K_{b_{\infty}})=\mathfrak{g}_{b_{\infty}}\cap\mathfrak{k}.

By assumption,
G_{b_{\infty}}
is a reductive group with maximal compact sub-group
K_{b_{\infty}}
so
\mathfrak{g}_{b_{\infty}}
is the complexification of
\mathfrak{k}_{b_{\infty}}
. Let

W=V\cap\mathrm{im}(L_{b_{\infty}})^{\bot}\subset V,

and the germ of smooth map

\Psi:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle(\mathfrak{g}\cap\mathfrak{g}_{b_{\infty}}^{\bot}\times W,(0,0))&\longrightarrow&\displaystyle(V,b_{\infty})\\
\displaystyle(\xi,c)&\longmapsto&\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\xi}\cdot(b_{\infty}+c)\end{array}\right..

It verifies
d\Psi(0,0)(\xi,c)=L_{b_{\infty}}\xi+c
which is an isomorphism. Therefore, we may apply the inverse function theorem. There is a
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that for all
t\geqslant t_{0}
,

g(t)\cdot b=\Psi(\xi(t),c(t))=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\xi(t)}\cdot(b_{\infty}+c(t)),

where
t\mapsto(\xi(t),c(t))
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
and converges toward
(0,0)
. For all
s,t\geqslant t_{0}
, we have

b_{\infty}+c(t)=f_{s}(t)\cdot(b_{\infty}+c(s)),

(18)

where
f_{s}(t)=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-\xi(t)}g(t)g(s)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{\xi(s)}\in G
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
.

Let us introduce, for all
c_{0}\in W
,

\psi_{c_{0}}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}\times W&\longrightarrow&\displaystyle V\\
\displaystyle(\eta,c)&\longmapsto&\displaystyle L_{b_{\infty}+c_{0}}\eta-c\end{array}\right.,\qquad K_{c_{0}}=\{(\eta,L_{b_{\infty}+c_{0}}\eta)|\eta\in\mathfrak{g}_{b_{\infty}}\}.

The
\psi_{c_{0}}
are a complex linear maps which vary continuously with
c_{0}
.
\psi_{0}
is surjective and its kernel is
\ker(\psi_{0})=\mathfrak{g}_{b_{\infty}}\times\{0\}=K_{0}
, which has dimension
m=\dim(\mathfrak{g}_{b_{\infty}})
. By openness of surjectivity, if
c_{0}
is close enough to
0
,
\psi_{c_{0}}
is surjective so
\dim(\ker(\psi_{c_{0}}))=\dim(\ker(\psi_{0}))=m
.

Moreover, for all
c_{0}\in W
and all
\eta\in\mathfrak{g}_{b_{\infty}}
,
L_{b_{\infty}+c_{0}}\eta=L_{c_{0}}\eta\in W
because
W
is preserved by the action of
G_{b_{\infty}}
. We deduce that
K_{c_{0}}\subset\ker(\psi_{c_{0}})
. By equality of dimensions (if
c_{0}
is close enough to
0
),
\ker(\psi_{c_{0}})=K_{c_{0}}
.

Choose
s\geqslant t_{0}
large enough so for all
t\geqslant s
,
\ker(\psi_{c(t)})=K_{c(t)}
. When we differentiate (
18
), we obtain

c^{\prime}(t)=L_{f_{s}(t)\cdot(b_{\infty}+c(s))}(f_{s}^{\prime}(t)f_{s}(t)^{-1})=L_{b_{\infty}+c(t)}(f_{s}^{\prime}(t)f_{s}(t)^{-1}).

In other words,
(f_{s}^{\prime}(t)f_{s}(t)^{-1},c^{\prime}(t))\in\ker(\psi_{c(t)})=K_{c(t)}
. It implies that
f_{s}^{\prime}(t)f_{s}(t)^{-1}\in\mathfrak{g}_{b_{\infty}}
. Since
f_{s}(s)=\mathrm{Id}_{E}\in G_{b_{\infty}}
, we deduce that for all
t\geqslant s
,
f_{s}(t)\in G_{b_{\infty}}
. Therefore, (
18
) becomes

c(t)=f_{s}(t)\cdot c(s).

It means that all the
c(t)
lie in the same
G_{b_{\infty}}
-orbit and
c(t)\underset{t\rightarrow+\infty}{\longrightarrow}0
. By
[
24
, Theorem 12.4]
, there is a
\xi
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\mathfrak{k}_{b_{\infty}}\subset\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}\mathfrak{k}
and a
c=g\cdot c(s)\in G_{b_{\infty}}\cdot c(s)
such that

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t\xi}\cdot c\underset{t\rightarrow+\infty}{\longrightarrow}0.

Finally,

b_{\infty}+c=g\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-\xi(s)}g(s)\cdot b\in G\cdot b,\qquad\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t\xi}\cdot(b_{\infty}+c)=b_{\infty}+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{t\xi}\cdot c\underset{t\rightarrow+\infty}{\longrightarrow}b_{\infty}.

∎

Proposition
5.13
will be useful in the semi-stable case
i.e.
when
\lim_{t\rightarrow+\infty}\mu_{\zeta}(\phi_{\zeta}(b,t))=0
(resp.
\lim_{t\rightarrow+\infty}\tilde{\mu}_{\zeta}(\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t))=0
). However, in the unstable case,
\mathrm{Stab}(b_{\infty})
may not be the complexification of
\mathrm{Stab}(b_{\infty})\cap K
so we can’t use the same argument. It may not even by reductive. We can still show an analogous result that will help us to construct destabilising sub-bundles, whose proof is widely inspired form
[
24
, Theorem 10.4]
.

b
b_{\infty}
t\mapsto\phi_{\zeta}(b,t)
\phi_{\zeta}(b,s_{0})
b_{0}
\phi_{\zeta}(b,s_{1})
\phi_{\zeta}(b,s_{2})
b_{1}
t\mapsto\mathbf{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathbf{\mathrm{i}}t\mu_{\zeta}(b_{\infty})}\cdot b_{0}
b_{2}
t\mapsto\mathbf{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathbf{\mathrm{i}}t\mu_{\zeta}(b_{\infty})}\cdot b_{1}
t\mapsto\mathbf{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathbf{\mathrm{i}}t\mu_{\zeta}(b_{\infty})}\cdot b_{2}

Figure 1:
Illustration of Proposition
5.14
. The dashed ellipses represent
K
-orbits.

Proposition 5.14
.

Let
b\in B_{2}
and
\zeta\in\mathcal{U}_{d}
such that
\phi_{\zeta}(b,t)
(resp.
\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,t)
) exists in
B_{2}
for all
t
and converges toward some point
b_{\infty}\in B_{2}
(resp.
\tilde{b}_{\infty}\in B_{2}
). Then there are sequences
s_{k}\rightarrow+\infty
(resp.
\tilde{s}_{k}\rightarrow+\infty
) and
b_{k}\in K\cdot\phi_{\zeta}(b,s_{k})
(resp.
\tilde{b}_{k}\in K\cdot\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}(b,\tilde{s}_{k})
) such that

\forall t\geqslant 0,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}t\mu_{\zeta}(b_{\infty})}\cdot b_{k}\in B_{2}\qquad\left(\textrm{resp. }\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}^{-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{i}}t\tilde{\mu}_{\zeta}(b_{\infty})}\cdot\tilde{b}_{k}\in B_{2}\right).

Moreover, it converges in
B_{2}
when
t\rightarrow+\infty
and
b_{k}\rightarrow b_{\infty}
(resp.
\tilde{b}_{k}\rightarrow\tilde{b}_{\infty}
).

Proof.

As before, we only prove it for
\tilde{\phi}_{\zeta}
because the proofs are rigorously the same.

Step 1 :
Introducing notations.

Let
M=G/K
and
\pi:G\rightarrow M
the canonical projection. As
\left<\cdot,\cdot\right>
on
\mathfrak{k}
is
K
